A proof of Onsager’s Conjecture for the SQG equation
Philip Isett, Sam Looi

Abstract
We construct solutions to the SQG equation that fail to conserve the Hamiltonian while having the maximal allowable regularity for this property to hold. This result solves the generalized Onsager conjecture on the threshold regularity for Hamiltonian conservation for SQG. We also prove nonuniqueness for arbitrary smooth data and an h-principle result.



1
Introduction

In this paper we are concerned with the surface quasi-geostrophic equation (SQG equation), which arises as an important model equation in geophysical fluid dynamics that has applications to both oceanic and meteorological flows
[
26
,
42
]
. The SQG equation for an unknown scalar field
\theta
on a two-dimensional spatial domain has the form

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta+\nabla\cdot(\theta u)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad u=T[\theta]=|\nabla|^{-1}\nabla^{\perp}\theta,

(1)

where
|\nabla|=\sqrt{-\Delta}
.

SQG is an
active scalar equation
, so called since the velocity field advecting the scalar field depends at every time on the values of the scalar field. The field
\theta
can represent either the temperature or surface buoyancy in a certain regime of stratified flow. The equation has been studied extensively in the mathematical
literature due to its close analogy with the 3D incompressible Euler equations and the problem of
blowup for initially classical solutions, which remains open as it does for the Euler equations. A survey of mathematical developments is given in the introduction to
[
7
]
. For more recent mathematical works on SQG we refer to
[
30
,
5
,
23
,
25
,
29
,
27
,
28
,
3
,
2
,
1
]
and the references therein.

Fundamental to the study of the SQG equation are the following basic conservation laws:

1.

For all sufficiently smooth solutions, the
Hamiltonian

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta(t,x))^{2}dx
remains constant.

2.

For all sufficiently smooth solutions, the
L^{p}
norms
\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}
remain constant for
1\leq p\leq\infty
, as do the integrals
\int F(\theta(t,x))dx
for any smooth function
F
.

3.

For all weak solutions to SQG, the mean, impulse, and angular momentum defined respectively by

\displaystyle M=\int\theta(t,x)dx,\quad\vec{I}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x\theta(t,x)dx,\quad A=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|^{2}\theta(t,x)dx

(2)

are conserved quantities. On the torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, the mean is well-defined and conserved.

(To prove (1), multiply the equation by
|\nabla|^{-1}\theta
and integrate by parts. To prove (2), use
\nabla\cdot u=0
to check that
F(\theta)
satisfies
\partial_{t}F(\theta)+\nabla\cdot(F(\theta)u)=0
. See
[
32
]
for a proof of (3).)

Note that in contrast to (3), the nonlinear laws (1) and (2) require that the solution is “sufficiently smooth”. If one expects that turbulent SQG solutions have a dual energy cascade as in the Batchelor-
Kraichnan predictions of 2D turbulence
[
14
,
15
,
7
]
, then one has motivation to consider weak solutions
that are not smooth. A basic question for the SQG equation is then: What is the minimal amount of smoothness required for the conservation laws to hold? This question is exactly the concern of the (generalized)
Onsager conjectures
for the SQG equation. A closely related open problem is to find the minimal regularity required to imply uniqueness of solutions.

Using Hölder spaces to measure regularity, the Onsager conjectures can be stated as follows

i)

If
\theta\in C^{0}
, then conservation of the Hamiltonian holds. However, for any
\alpha<1/2
, there exist solutions with
|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta\in L_{t}^{\infty}C^{\alpha}
that do not conserve the Hamiltonian.

ii)

If
\alpha>1/3
then the integral
\int F(\theta(t,x))dx
is conserved for any smooth function
F
. If
\alpha<1/3
, there exist solutions in
\theta\in L_{t}^{\infty}C^{\alpha}
that violate this conservation law.

The contribution of this paper is to fully answer the first conjecture in the affirmative.

Some remarks about these problems are in order:

1.

These problems generalize the original Onsager conjecture
[
41
]
, which concerned turbulent dissipation in the incompressible Euler equations and stated that the Hölder exponent 1/3 should mark the threshold regularity for conservation of energy for solutions to the incompressible Euler equations. See
[
20
,
22
]
for discussions of the significance of Onsager’s conjecture in turbulence theory.

2.

The threshold exponents are derived from the fact that the conservation law for sufficiently regular solutions has been proven in both of the above cases. Namely,
[
34
]
proves conservation of the Hamiltonian for solutions with
\theta\in L^{3}(I\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
, while
[
4
]
proves the conservation law (ii) for
\alpha>1/3
. The proofs are variants of the kinematic argument of
[
13
]
, which proved energy conservation for the Euler equations above Onsager’s conjectured threshold. For Hamiltonian conservation in the nonperiodic case, see
[
32
]
.

3.

Following the seminal work
[
19
]
, advances in the method of convex integration have made possible the pursuit of Onsager’s conjecture both for the Euler equations and more general fluid equations.
In particular, Onsager’s conjecture for the 3D Euler equations has been proven in
[
37
]
(see also

[
6
,
36
]
), while the first progress towards the Onsager conjecture (i) for SQG was made in
[
7
]
, with an alternative approach given in
[
31
]
. See
[
21
,
8
]
for surveys and
[
38
]
for a discussion of generalized Onsager conjectures.

4.

To make sense of the Onsager problem for the Hamiltonian, it must be noted that the SQG equation is well-defined for
\theta
having negative regularity.
Namely, for any smooth vector field
\phi(x)
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, the quadratic form

\displaystyle Q(\phi,\theta)

\displaystyle=\int\phi T[\theta]\cdot\nabla\theta dxdt,

initially defined for Schwartz
\theta
with compact frequency support away from the origin, has a unique bounded extension to
\theta\in\dot{H}^{-1/2}
. This fact, which relies on the anti-symmetry of the operator
T
, allows the SQG nonlinearity to be well-defined in
{\cal D}^{\prime}
for
\theta
of class
\theta\in L_{t}^{2}\dot{H}^{-1/2}
. In fact, one has the following estimate, which is optimal:

\displaystyle|Q(\phi,\theta)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathring{\nabla}^{2}_{j,\ell}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\theta\|_{\dot{H}^{-1/2}}^{2}

(3)

where
\mathring{\nabla}^{2}_{j\ell}\phi=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{j\ell}\Delta\phi
is the trace-free part of the Hessian of
\phi
. See
[
32
]
for a proof of this bound and its optimality, and
[
39
,
10
]
for earlier definitions of the nonlinearity with weaker estimates.

The main theorems of our work are the following, which settle the Onsager conjecture on the threshold for Hamiltonian conservation for SQG.

Theorem 1
.

For any
\alpha<1/2
, there exist weak solutions
\theta
to SQG that do not conserve the Hamiltonian such that
|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta\in C_{t}C^{\alpha}
.

Theorem 2

(Initial value problem)
.

For any
\alpha<1/2
,
c>0
and any smooth
\theta_{0}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
with integral zero there is a
T>0
and a weak solution to SQG of class
|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta\in C^{\alpha}
such that
\theta
realizes the initial data
\theta_{0}
at time
0
, coincides with the unique classical solution to SQG on
(-T,T)
, and is equal to
0
on
|t|\geq T+c
.

Theorem 3

(h-Principle)
.

For any
\alpha<1/2
and for any
C_{c}^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
function
f
that conserves the mean, i.e.
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}f(t,x)dx=0
for all
t
, there exists a sequence of SQG solutions
\theta_{n}
of class
|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta_{n}\in C_{t}C^{\alpha}
with compact support in time, such that

|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta_{n}\rightharpoonup|\nabla|^{-1/2}f
in
L_{t,x}^{\infty}
weak-*.

Theorems
2
and
3
, which both provide stronger forms of the first theorem, are both proven using a general approximation result (Theorem
4
below), which estimates the distance from a smooth SQG Reynolds flow in
C_{t}H^{-1/2}
to the space of weak solutions to SQG. The method of Theorem
2
can also be used to show that any two smooth SQG solutions can be glued together if they have the same mean. Theorem
3
, meanwhile, is inspired by the original h-Principle of Nash
[
40
]
on the
C^{0}
density of
C^{1}
isometric immersions in the space of short maps. The connection between h-principles and conservation laws was originally noted in
[
33
,
34
]
. See also
[
18
]
for a recent discussion of h-principle results in fluids. We refer to Section
9.1
for the proof of Theorem
2
and Section
9.2
for Theorem
3
.

The previous best known result on this problem, due to
[
7
]
, achieved regularity
|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta\in C^{3/10-}
, with an alternative approach given in
[
31
]
. Nonuniqueness of SQG steady states was proven in
[
10
]
.
We note also the works
[
9
,
17
]
, which prove nonuniqueness for
forced
SQG up to the Onsager threshold
|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta\in C^{1/2-}
.

Our improvement of the exponent relies on the following ideas:

1.

We build on the recent breakthrough solving the
2D
Onsager conjecture in
[
24
]
, which introduces a “Newton iteration,” which takes an arbitrary Euler-Reynolds flow and perturbs the velocity field so that the error is a sum of one-dimensional pieces with disjoint temporal support plus other error terms of acceptable size. This idea builds on work of
[
11
,
12
]
.

2.

The main difficulty in implementing the Newton iteration in the SQG context is to prove good estimates for a trace-free second-order
1
1

1

We require a second order anti-divergence since we base our approach on that of
[
31
]
.
anti-divergence tensor for the Newton correction. That is, a trace-free solution
\rho
to
\mbox{div div }\rho=w
, where
w
is the Newton correction. The straightforward estimate for the solution to this equation is
\|\rho\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim\|w\|_{C^{0}}
, which turns out to be far from adequate. Our approach to tackling this difficulty starts with the following idea:

•

We define a system of “transport elliptic” equations that couples the equation for
w
with an equation for a first-order anti-divergence
z
, which is then coupled to the equation for a second-order anti-divergence
r
(that may not be trace-free).

•

We use a Littlewood-Paley analysis to prove suitable estimates for
r
, which then are shown to imply suitable estimates for
\rho
. Much of the novelty of the work goes into this analysis.

3.

The second main difficulty that separates the SQG scheme from 2D Euler is that certain bilinear or quadratic terms that occur naturally in both the Newton iteration and the convex integration steps need to be written in divergence form with an anti-divergence that satisfies good (dimensionally correct) estimates. Here we build on ideas of
[
32
]
and provide a more direct approach to achieving such divergence forms. The main tool, which we call the “divergence form principle,” traces back to an important calculation in
[
7
]
that was generalized and streamlined in
[
31
]
. See Section
A.2
.

4.

Within the Newton iteration, we use analytical ideas that we believe to be of independent interest. For example, our methods can be used to give an alternative approach to some main results of
[
32
]
, including the conservation of angular momentum for general weak solutions, and our commutator estimates (e.g. Lemma
3.6
) can be used to give an alternative treatment of the low-frequency terms that arise in the improvement of the borderline regularity estimate sketched in
[
36
, Section 12]
. The sharp estimates we prove should be useful for obtaining an asymptotic endpoint type result for SQG, similar to that of
[
36
]
, but currently we do not know how to remove the reliance on double-exponential frequency growth in the Newton step.

While the above are the main ideas that are new to this paper, we note that they are not the only ones needed to surpass the exponent
|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta\in C^{3/10-}
. In particular, we rely on some nonperturbative techniques that were already used in
[
31
]
, including the use of nonlinear phase functions as in
[
35
]
, the microlocal Lemma of
[
34
]
and the bilinear microlocal Lemma of
[
31
]
. In
[
7
]
it was shown that certain perturbative techniques could be used in place of the above methods, but to get the sharp exponent we require techniques that remain effective on a nonperturbative timescale. We also take advantage of an observation in
[
9
]
, which is that estimates on pure time derivatives for SQG can be used in place of advective derivative bounds. While this point is likely not essential to the proof, it allows for a simpler argument where one does not need to commute advective derivatives with nonlocal operators many times.

Finally, we comment that during the writing of this paper we learned that
[
16
]
have independently and concurrently obtained another proof of Theorem
1
.

Acknowledgments
The work of PI has been supported by a Sloan Fellowship and the NSF grant DMS 2346799. The work of SL was supported by a Taussky–Todd Fellowship.

We now begin the proof with some notation.

1.1
Notation

In this paper, the dimension
d=2
. We use vectors to indicate multi-indices
\vec{a}
and use
|\vec{a}|
to indicate the order of the multi-index. For instance, if
\vec{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
,
1\leq a_{i}\leq d
, then
\nabla_{\vec{a}}=\nabla_{a_{1}}\nabla_{a_{2}}\nabla_{a_{3}}
is a partial derivative of order
|\vec{a}|=3
.
We will use many times the following elementary
counting inequality
with parameters
(x_{1},x_{2},y)
:

\displaystyle(x_{1}-y)_{+}+(x_{2}-y)_{+}

\displaystyle\leq(x_{1}+x_{2}-y)_{+}\quad\mbox{ if }x_{1},x_{2},y\geq 0.

(4)

We use the symbol
\overset{\sim}{\sum}
to indicate a sum with combinatorial coefficients that we have omitted to simplify notation. For example, the product rule implies,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{a}}(fg)=\overset{\sim}{\sum}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}f\nabla_{\vec{a}_{2}}g

(5)

where the sum runs over some but not all multi-indices with
|\vec{a}_{1}|+|\vec{a}_{2}|=|\vec{a}|
. Meanwhile, the chain rule and product rule give

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{a}}G(F(x))=\sum_{m=0}^{|\vec{a}|}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\partial^{m}G(F(x))\prod_{j=1}^{m}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{j}}F,

(6)

where the empty product is
1
and the sum is over certain multi-indices with
|\vec{a}_{1}|+\cdots+|\vec{a}_{m}|=|\vec{a}|
. (To be more precise the multi-indices should be of the form
\vec{a}_{m,j}
, but we omit the
m
subscript to simplify notation.)

We define Littlewood-Paley projections with the following conventions. Suppose
\hat{\eta}_{\leq 0}(\xi)
is
1
on
|\xi|\leq 1/2
and
0
on
1\leq|\xi|
,
\hat{\eta}_{\leq 0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. For
q\in\mathbb{Z}
we define

\displaystyle\widehat{P_{\leq q}f}(\xi)=\hat{\eta}(\xi/2^{q})\hat{f}(\xi).

Thus in physical space one has
P_{\leq q}f=\eta_{\leq q}\ast f
for
\eta_{\leq q}(h)=2^{dq}\eta_{\leq 0}(2^{q}h)
. We define Littlewood-Paley projections
P_{q}f=P_{\leq q+1}f-P_{\leq q}f
so that
P_{q}f
has frequency support in
\{2^{q-1}\leq|\xi|\leq 2^{q+1}\}
.

We use
P_{\approx q}
to indicate a Fourier multiplier that is a bump function adapted to frequencies of size
\xi\sim 2^{q}
. So for example,
P_{q}=P_{q}P_{\approx q}
.

We will use the summation convention to sum over repeated indices. For example,
\nabla_{i}u^{i}
is the divergence of a vector field
u
.

We will make use of two different anti-divergence operators. The first is the order
-1
operator
{\cal R}_{a}^{j\ell}
, which solves

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}{\cal R}_{a}^{j\ell}[f^{a}]

\displaystyle=f^{\ell},\qquad\delta_{j\ell}{\cal R}_{a}^{j\ell}=0,\quad{\cal R}^{j\ell}_{a}={\cal R}^{\ell j}_{a}

whenever
f^{\ell}
is a vector field of mean zero on the torus. The second operator is the order
-2
operator
{\cal R}^{j\ell}
, which solves

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}{\cal R}^{j\ell}[f]

\displaystyle=f,\qquad\delta_{j\ell}{\cal R}^{j\ell}=0,\quad{\cal R}^{j\ell}={\cal R}^{\ell j}

whenever
f
is a scalar field of mean zero on the torus. Explicit formulas for these operators can be given in terms of the Helmholtz projection to divergence-free vector fields

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\ell}_{a}

\displaystyle\equiv\delta_{a}^{\ell}-\Delta^{-1}\nabla^{\ell}\nabla_{a}

\displaystyle{\cal R}^{j\ell}_{a}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{-1}(\nabla^{j}\mathcal{H}_{a}^{\ell}+\nabla^{\ell}\mathcal{H}_{a}^{j})-\Delta^{-1}\delta^{j\ell}\nabla_{a}+2\Delta^{-2}\nabla^{j}\nabla^{\ell}\nabla_{a}

\displaystyle{\cal R}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=-\Delta^{-1}\delta^{j\ell}+2\Delta^{-2}\nabla^{j}\nabla^{\ell}

See Section
A.3
for a glossary of the various symbols introduced in the proof.

2
The main lemma

Definition 2.1
.

A scalar-valued
\theta:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and a symmetric traceless tensor field
R^{j\ell}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
solve the
SQG Reynolds equations
if

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta+u^{\ell}\nabla_{\ell}\theta

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}R^{j\ell}

\displaystyle u^{\ell}

\displaystyle=T^{\ell}\theta=\epsilon^{\ell a}\nabla_{a}|\nabla|^{-1}\theta

where
|\nabla|=\sqrt{-\Delta}
. The tensor
R^{j\ell}
is called the
error
since one has a solution when
R=0
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(\theta,u,R)
be an SQG-Reynolds flow,
\Xi\geq 1
and
\mathrm{D}_{u}\geq\mathrm{D}_{R}\geq 0
be non-negative numbers. Define the advective derivative
D_{t}:=\partial_{t}+T^{\ell}\theta\nabla_{\ell}
. We say that
(\theta,u,R)
has
frequency energy levels
below
(\Xi,\mathrm{D}_{u},\mathrm{D}_{R})
to order
L
in
C^{0}
if
(\theta,u,R)
are of class
C_{t}^{0}C_{x}^{L}
and the following statements hold

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\theta\|_{C^{0}},\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}u\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}e_{u}^{1/2},\qquad\mbox{ for all }|\vec{a}|=0,\ldots,L

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}R\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\mathrm{D}_{R},\qquad\mbox{ for all }|\vec{a}|=0,\ldots,L

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}D_{t}\theta\|_{C^{0}},\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}D_{t}u\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})e_{u}^{1/2}\qquad\mbox{ for all }|\vec{a}|=0,\ldots,L-1

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}D_{t}R\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})\mathrm{D}_{R},\qquad\mbox{ for all }|\vec{a}|=0,\ldots,L-1

with
e_{u}^{1/2}=\Xi^{1/2}\mathrm{D}_{u}^{1/2}
and
e_{R}^{1/2}=\Xi^{1/2}\mathrm{D}_{R}^{1/2}
. We note that, in contrast to other equations such as Euler,
e_{u}
and
e_{R}
will be large parameters.

Lemma 2.1

(Main Lemma)
.

For
L\geq 7
,
\eta\in(0,1)
there is a constant
\widehat{C}=\widehat{C}_{L,\eta}>1
such that the following holds: Given an SQG-Reynolds flow
(\theta,u,R)
with frequency energy levels below
(\Xi,D_{u},D_{R})
to order
L
and a set
J_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
with
\mathrm{supp}_{t}\,R\subseteq J_{0}
, suppose that
N\geq 1
satisfies

\displaystyle N\geq\widehat{C}N^{\frac{6}{L}}N^{4\eta}\Xi^{4\eta}\left(D_{u}/D_{R}\right).

(7)

Then there exists an SQG-Reynolds flow
(\accentset{\ast}{\theta},\accentset{\ast}{u},\accentset{\ast}{R})
of the form
\accentset{\ast}{\theta}=\theta+W,\accentset{\ast}{u}=u+T[W]
with frequency energy levels below

\displaystyle(\accentset{\ast}{\Xi},\accentset{\ast}{D}_{u},\accentset{\ast}{D}_{R})=(\widehat{C}N\Xi,D_{R},\widehat{C}N^{-1/2}(D_{R}/D_{u})^{1/2}D_{R})

(8)

to order
L
in
C^{0}
.

Furthermore the new stress
\accentset{\ast}{R}
and the correction
W
are supported in the set

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}_{t}\,\accentset{\ast}{R}\cup\mathrm{supp}_{t}\,W\subseteq N(J_{0}):=\{t+h~:~t\in J_{0},|h|\leq 5(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}\}

(9)

Additionally,
|\nabla|^{-1/2}W
satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}|\nabla|^{-1/2}W\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{C}(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\mathrm{D}_{R}^{1/2},\qquad|\vec{a}|=0,1.

(10)

It will be convenient to introduce the notation
\widehat{N}=N^{1/L}
. We have
\accentset{\ast}{\Xi}=\widehat{C}N\Xi
and
\accentset{\ast}{e}_{u}^{1/2}=\accentset{\ast}{\Xi}^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}
.

Remark 1
.

We remark that, while the lemma is true in the full range of parameters stated (and vacuously true if there is no
N
satisfying (
7
)), we will be applying the lemma in the regime where
\frac{6}{L}
and
4\eta
are both small parameters depending on the regularity of the limiting solution. In this case
N^{\frac{6}{L}+4\eta}\Xi^{4\eta}
in (
7
) is close to
1
so that (
7
) reduces to essentially
N\geq D_{u}/D_{R}
. This last estimate is basically equivalent to the blowup of
C^{0}
norms during the iteration, since
\|\theta\|_{C^{0}}\leq\Xi^{1/2}D_{u}^{1/2}
while the new scalar has size
\|\accentset{\ast}{\theta}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim N^{1/2}\Xi^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}
.

On the other hand, the
C^{0}
norms blow up only slowly as the regularity of our solutions becomes arbitrarily close to
\theta\in C^{0}
, so there exists only a small amount of room in the inequality
N\geq D_{u}/D_{R}
for (
7
) to be possible. Consequently
\frac{6}{L}
and
4\eta
must be small as the regularity of solutions approaches
C^{0}
, and verifying the inequality (
7
) in Section
9
is ultimately a delicate calculation.

Remark 2
.

We remark that the constant
\widehat{C}
in the bound on
\accentset{\ast}{D}_{R}
in (
8
) can be normalized to
1
as in previous work by applying the lemma with
N
chosen to be
\widehat{C}^{2}N
.

Remark 3
.

To prepare for proving Lemma
2.1
it is convenient to introduce the
final weighted norm

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[F]

\displaystyle=\max_{0\leq r\leq 1}\max_{0\leq|\vec{a}|+r\leq L}\frac{\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\accentset{\ast}{D}_{t}^{r}F\|}{\accentset{\ast}{\Xi}^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}}

(11)

where
\accentset{\ast}{D}_{t}=\partial_{t}+\accentset{\ast}{u}\cdot\nabla
is the new advective derivative and the new frequency and inverse timescale are
\accentset{\ast}{\Xi}=\hat{C}N\Xi
and
\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}=\accentset{\ast}{\Xi}\accentset{\ast}{e}_{u}^{1/2}
, with
\accentset{\ast}{e}_{u}^{1/2}=\accentset{\ast}{\Xi}^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}
. Including the factors of
\widehat{C}
in the definition of the norm is optional, since in any case one obtains an equivalent norm.

In this notation, the estimates we desire for
\accentset{\ast}{R}
in (
8
) can be written succinctly as

\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[\accentset{\ast}{R}]\lesssim N^{-1/2}(D_{R}/D_{u})^{1/2}D_{R}

with an implicit constant independent of
(\Xi,D_{u},D_{R})
and
N
. Some advantages of introducing such a weighted norm are that they are well-behaved with respect to products and composition with smooth functions, and the advective derivative
\accentset{\ast}{D}_{t}
can be replaced by other time derivatives that appear in the proof such as
D_{t}=\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla
or
\partial_{t}
to yield an equivalent norm that can be easier to control directly. We introduce these equivalent norms, denoted by

\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[F]\lesssim H^{*}[F]\lesssim\bar{H}^{*}[F]\lesssim\tilde{H}^{*}[F]\lesssim\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[F],

in Definition
5.1
.

2.1
Summary Section

The purpose of this section is to write down where all the main estimates of the paper are proven.

The main Proposition in the Newton step, which summarizes all that is needed to proceed to the convex integration step, is given by Proposition
3.1
. After the Newton step is setup, a summary section for the Newton step estimates appears in Section
3.6
. A key upshot is that the standard estimates for beginning a convex integration scheme hold once the Newton step is complete, but with the threshold for borrowed derivatives
L
replaced by
{\underline{L}}\equiv L-3
due to a loss of three derivatives in the Newton step equations.

The new frequency-energy levels for
\accentset{\ast}{\theta}
and
\accentset{\ast}{u}
are verified in Proposition
5.2
. The stress
\accentset{\ast}{R}
on the other hand has many different components, and each one is estimated either by
N^{-1}D_{R}
or the slightly larger quantity
N^{-1/2}(D_{u}/D_{R})^{-1/2}D_{R}
. The bounds for the mollification and quadratic errors in the Newton Step are consequences of Proposition
3.7
thanks to Proposition
5.1
, which guarantees the equivalence of estimates for
\partial_{t}
and
\accentset{\ast}{D}_{t}
for the final weighted norm. After
\Gamma
iterations of the Newton step, the acceptable bound for the error
R_{(\Gamma)}^{j\ell}
follows from Proposition
3.1
thanks to the choice of
\Gamma
in line (
30
) guaranteeing
D_{R,(\Gamma)}\leq N^{-1}D_{R}
, and also Proposition
5.1
, which guarantees that estimates for different advective derivatives are equivalent for the final weighted norm.

The error terms in the convex integration step are defined in line (
224
). The bounds for the transport error
R_{T}
and the high frequency interference terms
R_{H}
are obtained Section
8.1
using again the equivalence of weighted norms for different advective derivatives. The bounds for the mollification error
R_{M}
are obtained in Section
8.3
. The bounds for
R_{S}
, which contains the stress error and flow error, are obtained in Section
7
.

The bound (
10
) is a consequence of (
71
) and (
212
) where
\lambda\sim N\Xi
. Meanwhile, the bound (
9
) is a consequence of (
78
), the containment (
32
), and the construction of
e_{n}^{1/2}(t)
in line (
33
), since the support of the convex integration perturbation and error are bounded by the support of
e_{n}^{1/2}(t)
.

3
The Newton Step

Consider a given SQG-Reynolds flow
(\theta,u,R)
with frequency energy levels below
(\Xi,\mathrm{D}_{u},\mathrm{D}_{R})
to order
L
and time support interval
J_{0}
and let
\eta>0
be given. Our goal is to perturb the scalar field in such a way that the error will become smaller. This goal will be achieved in two steps, the first called the Newton step and the second called the convex integration step. Our new scalar field
\accentset{\ast}{\theta}
will have the form
\theta+w+\Theta
, where
w
is called the
Newton perturbation
and
\Theta
is called the
oscillatory perturbation
, which arises in the convex integration step.

The goal of the Newton perturbation is to perturb the scalar field so that the original stress
R
is replaced by a new
\tilde{R}
that is supported on disjoint intervals, where in each interval
\tilde{R}
can be canceled out by a one-dimensional convex integration perturbation. Doing so overcomes the difficulty in the convex integration step that waves oscillating in distinct directions are not allowed to interfere with each other.

Constructing the Newton perturbation that achieves this localization will be achieved in a number
\Gamma=\lceil\eta^{-1}\rceil
iterative steps indexed by
n\in\{0,\ldots,\Gamma-1\}
. After the Newton perturbation we will add a high frequency perturbation
\Theta
that will be the sum of waves of the form
\Theta=\sum_{I}\Theta_{I}\approx\sum_{I}\theta_{I}e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}
that will cancel out the “low frequency part” of what remains of the error, leaving behind an error that is small enough for the whole procedure to be repeated until the error is reduced to zero in the limit. Each wave has a conjugate wave
\Theta_{\bar{I}}=\overline{\Theta}_{I}
,
\xi_{\bar{I}}=-\xi_{I}
, making
\Theta
real-valued.

We define the sets
F=\{\pm(1,2),\pm(2,1)\}
and
\mathbb{F}=\{(1,2),(2,1)\}
, which will be the directions in which the oscillatory waves of the convex integration stage oscillate. That is,
\nabla\xi_{I}
is reasonably (
O(1)
) close to an element of
F
.

During the convex integration step, each wave
\Theta_{I}+\Theta_{\bar{I}}
is individually able to cancel out a “one-dimensional” component of the error that takes on the form
-\gamma^{2}B^{j\ell}(\nabla\xi_{I})
, where

\displaystyle B^{j\ell}(p)=-i(\nabla^{j}m^{\ell}(p)+\nabla^{\ell}m^{j})(p),

(12)

where
m^{\ell}(p)~=~i\epsilon^{\ell a}p_{a}|p|^{-1}
is the multiplier for SQG and where
\gamma
is a slowly varying smooth function that remains to be chosen. The leading symbol and its remainder are supplied by the Bilinear Microlocal Lemma
[
31
, Lemma 4.2]
; the applicability of that lemma in our notation is explained after (
226
). Thus one of the first tasks that must be done is to decompose the (low frequency part of the) error into a linear combination of terms of this form. Before we perform this decomposition, we must define what we mean by the low frequency part of the error, which is the part that will be canceled out by the oscillatory perturbation
\Theta
.

3.1
Length scales and time scales

The construction involves three principle length scales, which are all small parameters. The ambient length scale is
\Xi^{-1}
, the mollification length scale is
\epsilon=\epsilon_{x}=N^{-1/L}\Xi^{-1}=\widehat{N}^{-1}\Xi^{-1}
, and the new length scale is
(N\Xi)^{-1}
. Closely related are the cutoff frequency
\widehat{\Xi}=\widehat{N}\Xi=N^{1/L}\Xi
and the new frequency, a number
\lambda\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
such that
\lambda\sim N\Xi
. The chain of inequalities
1\ll N^{1/L}=\widehat{N}\ll N\lesssim\widehat{C}N
imply an ordering of the inverse length scales

\displaystyle\Xi\ll\epsilon^{-1}=\widehat{\Xi}\ll\lambda\lesssim\accentset{\ast}{\Xi}.

(13)

The important time scales in the problem include the ambient time scale
(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}
, the slow time scale for the Newton iteration

\displaystyle\tau\equiv b(\log\widehat{\Xi})^{-1}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}=b(\log\widehat{\Xi})^{-1}(\Xi^{3/2}\mathrm{D}_{u}^{1/2})^{-1},

(14)

(where
b
is an
(L,\eta)
-dependent constant to be chosen). Many estimates will have the feature that the advective derivative costs
\tau^{-1}
. For bookkeeping purposes, we introduce the auxiliary energy level

\displaystyle\widetilde{e}_{u}^{1/2}:=(\log\widehat{\Xi})e_{u}^{1/2},\qquad\Xi\widetilde{e}_{u}^{1/2}=b\tau^{-1}\sim_{L,\eta}\tau^{-1}.

(15)

The fast time scale for the Newton iteration is
\mu^{-1}
, where

\displaystyle\mu=N^{1/2}\Xi e_{R}^{1/2}=N^{1/2}\Xi^{3/2}\mathrm{D}_{R}^{1/2},

(16)

The timescale for mollification along the flow is
\epsilon_{t}
, where

\displaystyle\epsilon_{t}

\displaystyle=(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}(D_{u}/D_{R})^{-1/2}N^{-1/2}.

(17)

The new time scale is
\accentset{\ast}{\tau}=(\accentset{\ast}{\Xi}\accentset{\ast}{e}_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}
.

The order of the associated inverse time scales is

\displaystyle\Xi e_{u}^{1/2}\ll\tau^{-1}\ll\mu\ll\epsilon_{t}^{-1}\ll\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}.

(18)

For the last comparison, observe that

\epsilon_{t}^{-1}/\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}\lesssim(D_{u}/D_{R})/N
,
which is small due to (
7
).

Finally, the characteristic velocities
e_{u}^{1/2}
and
\accentset{\ast}{e}_{u}^{1/2}
satisfy
e_{u}^{1/2}\ll\accentset{\ast}{e}_{u}^{1/2}
using
N\gg D_{u}/D_{R}
. This comparison is different from Euler, where the characteristic velocities get smaller at smaller scales, and reflects the fact that the velocity field in the limit is not bounded.

3.2
Regularizing the scalar field and error tensor

We mollify the velocity field and scalar at the length scale
\epsilon=(\widehat{N}\Xi)^{-1}=\widehat{\Xi}^{-1}
. Choose an integer
q_{\epsilon}
such that
2^{q_{\epsilon}}
is within a factor of
2
of
\epsilon^{-1}
and set

\displaystyle\theta_{\epsilon}=P_{\leq q_{\epsilon}}\theta=\eta_{\leq q_{\epsilon}}\ast\theta,\qquad u_{\epsilon}^{\ell}=T^{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon},

(19)

where
P_{\leq q_{\epsilon}}
is a Littlewood–Paley projection in the spatial variables. Its convolution kernel has integral one and all moments of orders
1
through
L
equal to zero.

In terms of the coarse scale velocity field we define the coarse scale advective derivative according to

\overline{D}_{t}=\partial_{t}+u_{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla.

The error tensor
R
must also be regularized before we attempt to cancel it out. We define
R_{\epsilon}
by mollifying
\eta_{\epsilon_{x}}\ast_{x}\eta_{\epsilon_{x}}\ast_{x}R(t,x)
only in the spatial variables at the length scale
\epsilon_{x}=N^{-1/L}\Xi^{-1},
and using a mollifying kernel such that
\int h^{\vec{a}}\eta(h)dh=0
for all multi-indices
1\leq|\vec{a}|\leq L
.

To define the iteration we need the following basic estimates that follow from Minkowski’s inequality

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}}\leq A_{L}\Xi e_{u}^{1/2},\qquad\|R_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}}\leq A_{L}D_{R}

(20)

where
A_{L}=\max\{\|\eta_{\leq q_{\epsilon}}\|_{L^{1}},\|\eta_{\epsilon_{x}}\ast\eta_{\epsilon_{x}}\|_{L^{1}}\}
.

3.3
Prelude to convex integration

Let
e_{0}(t)
be a smooth function of time (an “envelope function”) to be chosen shortly. We re-write the SQG-Reynolds equation as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta+\nabla_{\ell}[\theta T^{\ell}[\theta]]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}(R_{\epsilon}^{j\ell}-e_{0}(t)M^{j\ell})+\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}(R^{j\ell}-R_{\epsilon}^{j\ell})

(21)

where

\displaystyle M^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=B^{j\ell}((1,2))+B^{j\ell}((2,1))

(22)

Here we use that
M^{j\ell}
is a constant matrix, which implies
\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}M^{j\ell}=0
. The function
e_{0}(t)
will be chosen just large enough so that
e_{0}(t)M^{j\ell}
dominates the term
R_{\epsilon}^{j\ell}
.

We must decompose
R_{\epsilon}-e_{0}M
into components that live on a size
\tau
time interval and that can be canceled out by one-dimensional oscillations. Towards the latter goal, consider the quadratic equation for unknown coefficients
\gamma_{f}
,
f\in\mathbb{F}=\{(1,2),(2,1)\}
, where the
p_{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\varepsilon^{j\ell}
is a symmetric and trace-free two-tensor:

\displaystyle\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}}\gamma_{f}^{2}B^{j\ell}(p_{f})

\displaystyle=M^{j\ell}-\varepsilon^{j\ell}.

(23)

When each
p_{f}=f
and
\varepsilon=0
, this equation has a solution given by
\gamma_{(1,2)}=1
and
\gamma_{(2,1)}=1
. Moreover, the tensors
B^{j\ell}((1,2))
and
B^{j\ell}((2,1))
are easily checked to be linearly independent, hence form a basis for the two-dimensional space of symmetric, trace-free two-tensors on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Moreover,
B^{j\ell}(p)
depends smoothly on
p
, so by the implicit function theorem (or simply inverting the linear equation and taking square roots) there is a constant
c_{1}>0
and there are smooth functions
\gamma_{f}(\varepsilon,p)
of
(\varepsilon,p_{(1,2)},p_{(2,1)})
,
f\in\mathbb{F}
, defined in an open neighborhood of the set

\displaystyle|\varepsilon|\leq c_{1},|p_{f}-f|\leq c_{1}

(24)

such that the
\gamma_{f}(\varepsilon,p)
solve (
23
) and take values in
|\gamma_{f}(\varepsilon,p)-1|\leq 1/2
. (The choice of norms in (
24
) is unimportant since all norms in finite dimensions are equivalent.)

We now choose
e_{0}(t)
to make sure
e_{0}(t)M^{j\ell}
dominates
R_{\epsilon}^{j\ell}
. Let
A_{L}
be as in (
20
). Let
M_{e}
denote a constant depending on
L
, to be chosen, such that
e_{0}(t)
takes on the constant value
M_{e}D_{R}
on the set
\{t+h~:~t\in J_{0},|h|\leq(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}\}
, which contains the support of
R_{\epsilon}
and also the support of
\check{R}
to be defined later on.

We now decompose the right hand side of (
21
) into time-localized pieces that are almost well-prepared to be canceled by the convex integration construction. By a standard construction (see for instance
[
19
]
), we can choose a partition in unity in time of the form

1=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\chi_{k}^{2}(t),\qquad\chi_{k}(t)=\chi(\tau^{-1}(t-\tau_{k}))

where each
\chi_{k}(t)
has support in
(\tau k-\tau,\tau k+\tau)
and is equal to
1
in
[\tau k-\tau/4,\tau_{k}+\tau/4]
.

Define the Newton step phase functions as the solutions to

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\partial_{t}+u_{\epsilon}^{j}\nabla_{j})\check{\xi}_{(k,f)}&=0\\
\check{\xi}_{(k,f)}(k\tau,x)&=f\cdot x.\end{split}

(25)

(We note that
\nabla\check{\xi}
is well-defined on the torus thanks to the condition
f\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.) These oscillate in the direction
\nabla\xi_{(k,f)}
, which solves the transport equation

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+u_{\epsilon}^{j})\nabla_{j}\nabla_{a}\check{\xi}_{(k,f)}

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{a}u_{\epsilon}^{j}\nabla_{j}\check{\xi}_{(k,f)}

\displaystyle\nabla\check{\xi}_{(k,f)}(\tau k,x)

\displaystyle=f

Recalling that
\|\nabla u_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}}\leq A_{L}\Xi e_{u}^{1/2}
, Gronwall’s inequality yields

|\nabla\check{\xi}_{(k,f)}-f|\leq|f|[\exp(A_{L}\Xi e_{u}^{1/2}|t-\tau k|)-1]

and hence (compare
[
35
, Proposition 17.2, equations (536)–(537)]
)

\displaystyle|\nabla\check{\xi}_{(k,f)}-f|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{L}b,

(26)

for times of the order
|t-\tau k|\leq\tau\leq b(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}
,
where
b
is the constant in the definition of
\tau
.

Now define the coefficients for stage
0
of the Newton step

\displaystyle\gamma_{(k,f,0)}=\chi_{k}e_{0}^{1/2}(t)\gamma_{f}\left(\frac{R_{\epsilon}^{j\ell}}{M_{e}D_{R}},\nabla\check{\xi}_{k}\right)

(27)

where
\nabla\check{\xi}_{k}=(\nabla\check{\xi}_{(1,2)},\nabla\check{\xi}_{(2,1)})
. The coefficients are well defined provided
b
is sufficiently small and
M_{e}
is sufficiently large (both depending on
A_{L}
) so that the arguments of
\gamma_{f}
lie in the neighborhood (
24
). We can fix
M_{e}
once and for all so that not only is (
27
) indeed well-defined, but we have some room so that
\|R_{\epsilon}/(M_{e}D_{R})\|_{C^{0}}\leq A_{L}/M_{e}
is bounded by
c_{1}/2
, where
c_{1}
is as in (
24
). Fix also an upper bound for
b
so that the phase gradients lie in (
24
) with
c_{1}/2
replacing
c_{1}
.

In terms of these coefficients, we finally have the decomposition into time-localized pieces that are almost prepared for convex integration as desired

\displaystyle e_{0}(t)M^{j\ell}-R_{\epsilon}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}}A_{(k,f,0)}^{j\ell}

(28)

\displaystyle A_{(k,f,0)}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{(k,f,0)}^{2}B^{j\ell}(\nabla\check{\xi}_{(k,f)}).

(29)

Each of the individual terms in the sum (
28
) is now exactly of the form that can be canceled by a one dimensional wave using convex integration. At this moment the difficulty we face is the interference problem: Waves that oscillate in different directions interact with each other and produce terms that cannot be well-controlled.

The solution to this problem, building on
[
24
,
12
]
, is to use a
Newton iteration
, which perturbs the velocity field in such a way that the individual terms in (
28
) are replaced by terms with disjoint support in time, still well-prepared for convex integration, modulo other acceptable error terms. The challenge we face in the present work is how to adapt this method, originally used for 2D Euler, to the setting of SQG.

3.4
Setting up the Newton iteration

Once again, our aim is to add a Newton correction
w
to
\theta
that will replace the term
(R_{\epsilon}^{j\ell}-e_{0}(t)M^{j\ell})
with a sum of error terms that are “one-dimensional” with disjoint supports that can be canceled out by a convex integration argument, modulo other acceptable errors. This task will be done by an iteration involving
\Gamma=\Gamma(\eta)
steps indexed by
n\in\{0,\ldots,\Gamma-1\}
.

We write down some basic intuition for the Newton iteration of
[
24
]
in the current context. We want to replace the forcing terms
\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}A_{(k,f,0)}^{j\ell}
with forcing terms that have disjoint support. For each
([k],f)\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{F}
choose a one-periodic function
g_{([k],f,0)}
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}g_{([k],f,0)}^{2}(y)dy=1
and such that the supports of the
g_{([k],f,0)}
are disjoint. Then, at least on time scales of size
\tau
, one suspects that replacing each forcing term
\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}A_{(k,f,0)}^{j\ell}
with the forcing term
g_{([k],f,0)}^{2}(\mu t)\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}A_{(k,f,0)}^{j\ell}
will only perturb our approximate solution
\theta
since the two forcing terms are essentially the same on average in time; in other words, the two forcing terms differ by something high frequency in time as
\mu\gg\tau^{-1}
. And if we believe the change in
\theta
will be small, we can hope the error terms generated by nonlinear interactions are acceptably small errors for the iteration when
\mu
is large. Moreover, with this choice the new stress terms
g_{([k],f,0)}^{2}(\mu t)A_{(k,f,0)}^{j\ell}
have disjoint supports and are well-prepared for convex integration, as the term
g_{([k],f,0)}^{2}
can be absorbed into the coefficients
\gamma^{2}
. While this intuition holds on short time intervals, we can also hope that the corrections can be glued together on size
\tau
time intervals in such a way that the gluing error is also under control similar to the gluing in the proof of
[
37
]
. This last task involves nontrivial harmonic analysis that is a main undertaking of this paper.

There is an important point, however, that the above intuition neglects. According to the above, a large choice of
\mu
should result in a small perturbation to the scalar
\theta
and acceptable error terms, but there is a price to pay from taking a large
\mu
which is that the transport term in the convex integration becomes larger by a factor of
\mu
, The best one can do is optimize between the nonlinear errors in the Newton step and the transport error in convex integration, which has resulted in our choice of
\mu
in (
16
). It turns out that, with this choice, the gain in the gluing error is miniscule (the new error is not much smaller than
D_{R,1}=D_{R}/(N\Xi)^{\eta}
), but this Newton step can be iterated
O(\eta^{-1})
times until the gluing error is acceptable and convex integration can finally be performed.

Let us now prepare for the full Newton iteration. Choose cutoff functions
\tilde{\chi}_{k}(t)=\tilde{\chi}(\tau^{-1}(\tau k-t))
with support in
|t-\tau_{k}|\leq 3\tau/2
such that
\tilde{\chi}_{k}
is
1
on the support of
\chi_{k}
. Set
D_{R,0}=D_{R}
and define the
n
th error bound

\displaystyle D_{R,n}=(N^{-\eta}\Xi^{-\eta})^{n}D_{R,0},

(30)

Fix
\Gamma=\Gamma(\eta)
such that
D_{R,\Gamma}\leq D_{R}/N
.

With
J_{0}
as in the Main Lemma, the
n
th error will be supported in

\displaystyle J_{n+1}=\{t+h~:~t\in J_{n}\,|h|\leq 3\tau\}.

(31)

Fix
b=b(\eta,L)
such that

\displaystyle J_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{t+h~:~t\in J_{0}\,|h|\leq(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}\}.

(32)

We choose functions
e_{n}(t)
supported in a
\tau/2
neighborhood of
J_{n}
such that
e_{n}^{1/2}(t)
is smooth and satisfies the cutoff-scale estimates

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{r}e_{n}^{1/2}(t)\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim_{r}\tau^{-r}D_{R,n}^{1/2},

(33)

and
e_{n}(t)=M_{e}D_{R,n}
on a
\tau/4
neighborhood of
J_{n}
. Such an
e_{n}^{1/2}(t)
can be constructed by mollifying the indicator function of a
\tau/3
open neighborhood of
J_{n}
.

Define the index
J=(k,n)
taking values in
\mathbb{Z}\times\{0,1,\ldots,\Gamma-1\}
. The
n
th stress decomposes into terms of the form

\displaystyle A_{(f,k,n)}^{j\ell}=A_{(f,J)}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle:=\chi_{k}^{2}(t)e_{n}(t)\,\gamma_{f}^{2}\!\left(\frac{R_{(n)}}{M_{e}D_{R,n}},\nabla\check{\xi}_{k}\right)B^{j\ell}(\nabla\check{\xi}_{(k,f)}).

(34)

Except for the case
n=0
where
R_{0}=R_{\epsilon}
, we have not yet defined
R_{(n)}
but will do so shortly.

Towards obtaining stress terms with disjoint support, fix a choice of
4\Gamma
smooth non-negative
1
-periodic functions
g_{([k],f,n)}
indexed by
([k],f,n)\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{F}\times\{0,\ldots,\Gamma-1\}
such that their supports are disjoint and normalized so that
\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}g_{([k],f,n)}^{2}(y)dy=1
. The existence of such functions is a simple induction on the number of
g
(see for instance
[
24
, Lemma 3.3]
). For
J=(k,n)
we abbreviate
[J]=([k],n)\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\{0,\ldots,\Gamma-1\}
.

Define
w_{J}
to solve the forced linear transport equation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}w_{J}+T^{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}\nabla_{\ell}w_{J}+T^{\ell}w_{J}\nabla_{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}&=\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}}(1-g_{[f,J]}^{2}(\mu t))\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}A_{(f,J)}^{j\ell}\\
&=:\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}O_{J}^{j\ell}\\
w_{J}(k\tau,x)&=\tilde{w}_{J}(k\tau,x).\end{split}

(35)

The initial data is in terms of a parametrix
\tilde{w}_{(k,n)}
that we introduce in Section
3.5
below to prove cancellation in solving the equation. Using a contraction mapping argument in Sobolev spaces, we prove existence, uniqueness and smoothness of solutions to this equation for arbitrary smooth data and forcing in the Appendix section
A.1
.

Let
\theta_{n}
and
T^{\ell}\theta_{n}
refer to the scalar field and velocity field after
n
Newton iteration steps. As suggested above, we want to glue together the
w_{(k,n)}
to define our Newton correction. Thus,

\displaystyle\theta_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\theta+\sum_{0\leq j\leq n}w_{j},\qquad w_{n}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\chi}_{k}w_{(k,n)}.

The Newton correction is
w=\sum_{n=0}^{\Gamma-1}w_{n}
.

After
n
Newton steps, we inductively have a system of the form

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta_{n}+T^{\ell}\theta_{n}\nabla_{\ell}\theta_{n}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}R_{(n)}^{j\ell}+\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}S_{(n)}^{j\ell}+\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}P_{(n)}^{j\ell}

(36)

where

•

R_{(n)}
is the gluing error obtained in the previous stage if
n\geq 1
(see (
38
) below), while
R_{(0)}^{j\ell}=R_{\epsilon}^{j\ell}
.

•

S_{(n)}
is the error that will be canceled out by one-dimensional oscillations during the convex integration step while
S_{(0)}^{j\ell}=0
.

•

P_{(n)}
is the error that is small enough to be included in
\accentset{\ast}{R}
in the next stage of the iteration, where
P_{(0)}=R^{j\ell}-R_{\epsilon}^{j\ell}
.

More specifically: With such a choice of Newton correction, the errors after the
n+1
’th step solve the following system of equations

\displaystyle\theta_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\theta_{n}+w_{n}=\theta+\sum_{j=0}^{n}w_{j}

(37)

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}R_{(n+1)}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\partial_{t}\tilde{\chi}_{k}w_{(k,n)}

(38)

\displaystyle S_{(n+1)}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=S_{(n)}^{j\ell}-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}}g_{(f,[k],n)}^{2}(\mu t)A_{(f,k,n)}^{j\ell}

(39)

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}P_{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}P_{(n)}+T^{\ell}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})\nabla_{\ell}w_{n}+T^{\ell}w_{n}\nabla_{\ell}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})

(40)

\displaystyle+T^{\ell}w_{n}\nabla_{\ell}w_{n}+\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}(T^{\ell}w_{n}\nabla_{\ell}w_{j}+T^{\ell}w_{j}\nabla_{\ell}w_{n})

(41)

Notice that the terms in (
38
), (
40
) and (
41
) are not in the form of a second-order divergence of a trace-free tensor field, in contrast to the analogous terms for Euler, where the latter two are readily of the correct form. Handling this new issue and getting good estimates for the solutions to (
38
), (
40
)-(
41
) is another of the main contributions of this paper.

3.5
Obtaining cancellation in the Newton iteration

It is necessary to prove a great deal of cancellation in our estimates for the Newton corrections
w_{J}
. First, we must exploit the fact that the right hand side is high frequency in time when
\mu
is large if we hope to have adequate estimates for
P_{(n+1)}^{j\ell}
to be acceptable. To do so we must use bounds on the advective derivatives of the
A_{J}
terms to show that their characteristic time scale (which will be
\tau
) is larger than the fast time scale
\mu^{-1}
. But we also need another cancellation when we solve the divergence equation to determine
R_{(n+1)}^{j\ell}
. Indeed we should gain a factor of
\Xi^{-2}
when solving this second order equation to be consistent with dimensional analysis, which is a much better bound than the trivial one obtained from
\|\mbox{div}^{-1}\mbox{div}^{-1}f\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0}}
, and which is subtle because the terms
w_{J}
are not high frequency in space.

To obtain both of these cancellations at once, we combine two techniques. First, we use the technique of
[
24
]
to write each
w_{J}
as
w_{J}=\tilde{w}_{J}+\bar{w}_{J}
as a parametrix plus a remainder, where the parametrix is explicit and gains the smallness of
\mu^{-1}
while the remainder solves the transport equation with a forcing term that gains a
\mu^{-1}
. (This can be compared with the use of nonstationary phase in convex integration; see Section
8
.) Second, we combine this with the method of
[
37
]
to find a second order anti-divergence
r_{J}^{j\ell}
(not symmetric or trace free) that is defined through a “transport elliptic equation” and is shown by dynamical methods to satisfy good bounds. We then transfer these bounds for
r_{J}
with a logarithmic loss to
\rho_{J}^{j\ell}={\cal R}^{j\ell}w_{J}={\cal R}^{j\ell}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}r_{J}^{ab}
. The explicit details are as follows.

For
J=(k,n)
, consider a solution to the equation

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+T^{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}\nabla_{\ell})z_{J}^{i}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{a}T^{i}\theta_{\epsilon}z_{J}^{a}-T^{i}w_{J}\theta_{\epsilon}+\nabla_{a}O_{J}^{ia}

(42)

\displaystyle z_{J}^{i}(\tau k,x)

\displaystyle=\tilde{z}_{J}^{i}(\tau k)

(43)

with smooth initial data to be specified below such that
\nabla_{i}\tilde{z}_{(k,n)}^{i}(\tau k)=\tilde{w}_{(k,n)}(\tau k)
. The existence of a smooth solution to (
42
) follows from standard existence theory for transport equations by the method of characteristics and variation of parameters.

It is not difficult to check that if
z_{J}^{i}
solves (
42
), then
\nabla_{i}z_{J}^{i}
, the divergence of
z_{J}
, satisfies
\overline{D}_{t}\nabla_{i}z_{J}^{i}=\overline{D}_{t}w_{J}
and thus equals
w_{J}
as long as it does so initially. Thus
z_{J}^{j}
is an anti-divergence for
w_{J}
.

We now wish to find an anti-divergence for
z_{J}^{j}
. Using the fact that the divergence of
z_{J}^{j}
is
w_{J}
, we can rewrite equation (
42
) as

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+T^{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}\nabla_{\ell})z_{J}^{i}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\nabla_{a}T^{i}\theta_{\epsilon}z_{J}^{a}-\nabla_{a}T^{i}z_{J}^{a}\theta_{\epsilon}}_{\text{special term}}+\nabla_{a}O_{J}^{ia}

(44)

The special term has a structure that makes it possible to be put in divergence form. Ultimately the most important point is that the operator
\nabla_{a}T^{i}
has a symbol that is even (and degree 1 homogeneous) and the fact that a minus sign appears (which together imply that the special term has integral zero). Using these properties, we claim that

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}T^{i}[\theta_{\epsilon}]z_{J}^{a}-\nabla_{a}T^{i}[z_{J}^{a}]\theta_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}{\cal B}^{ji}_{a}[z_{J}^{a},\theta_{\epsilon}]

(45)

where
{\cal B}_{a}^{ji}
is a bilinear form that we will be able to estimate. In terms of this anti-divergence, (which will only appear explicitly when we estimate
r_{J}
) define
r_{J}^{ij}
to be the unique two-tensor solving

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+T^{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}\nabla_{\ell})r_{J}^{ij}

\displaystyle={\cal R}^{ij}_{b}\nabla_{\ell}[\nabla_{a}T^{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}r_{J}^{ab}]+{\cal B}^{ij}_{b}[z_{J}^{b},\theta_{\epsilon}]+O_{J}^{ij}

(46)

\displaystyle r_{J}^{ij}(\tau k,x)

\displaystyle=\tilde{r}_{J}^{ij}(\tau k,x),

(47)

where the initial data, to be specified shortly, satisfies
\nabla_{i}\tilde{r}_{(k,n)}^{ij}(\tau k)=\tilde{z}_{(k,n)}^{j}(\tau k)
. Here
{\cal R}^{ij}_{a}
is as defined in Section
1.1
. Note that any solution to the initial value problem (
46
) satisfies
\nabla_{i}r_{J}^{ij}=z_{J}^{j}
because the two vector fields have the same advective derivative and initial condition, and hence crucially
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{i}r_{J}^{ij}=w_{J}
. In the above we rely on the existence and uniqueness of smooth solutions of equations of the form

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v^{\ell}\nabla_{\ell})r^{ij}

\displaystyle={\cal R}^{ij}_{a}\nabla_{\ell}[\nabla_{b}v^{\ell}r^{ab}]+f^{ij},

(48)

\displaystyle r^{ij}(0,x)

\displaystyle=r_{0}^{ij}

(49)

when
v
is div-free and
v
,
f
and
r_{0}
are smooth in space. This well-posedness is proven by contraction mapping in Sobolev spaces in the Appendix
2
2

2

The cited reference proceeds in dimension
3
, but the only important properties used are that
{\cal R}_{a}^{ij}\nabla_{\ell}
commutes with partial derivatives and is bounded on
L^{p}
for
1<p<\infty
, which hold as well in dimension
2
.
of
[
37
]
.

Our description of the Newton correction is complete once we specify the decompositions of
w_{J}=\tilde{w}_{J}+\bar{w}_{J}
,
z_{J}^{i}=\tilde{z}_{J}^{i}+\bar{z}_{J}^{i}
and
r_{J}^{ij}=\tilde{r}_{J}^{ij}+\bar{r}_{J}^{ij}
into a parametrix part plus a remainder.

Let
h_{f,[J]}(T)=\int_{0}^{T}(1-g_{f,[J]}^{2}(s))ds
and decompose
w_{J}=\bar{w}_{J}+\tilde{w}_{J}
, where

\displaystyle\tilde{w}_{J}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}}\mu^{-1}h_{f,[J]}(\mu t)\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}A_{(f,J)}^{j\ell}

(50)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{w}_{J}+T^{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}\nabla_{\ell}\bar{w}_{J}

\displaystyle=-T^{\ell}w_{J}\nabla_{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}-\tilde{O}_{J}

(51)

\displaystyle\bar{w}_{J}(\tau k)

\displaystyle=0

(52)

\displaystyle\tilde{O}_{J}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}}\mu^{-1}h_{f,[J]}(\mu t)\overline{D}_{t}\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}A_{(f,J)}^{j\ell}

(53)

We also decompose
z_{J}=\bar{z}_{J}+\tilde{z}_{J}
, where

\displaystyle\tilde{z}_{J}^{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}}\mu^{-1}h_{f,[J]}(\mu t)\nabla_{j}A_{(f,J)}^{ij}

(54)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{z}_{J}^{i}+T^{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}\nabla_{\ell}\bar{z}_{J}^{i}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{a}T^{i}\theta_{\epsilon}z_{J}^{a}-T^{i}w_{J}\theta_{\epsilon}-\tilde{O}_{J}^{i}

(55)

\displaystyle\bar{z}_{J}^{i}(\tau k)

\displaystyle=0

(56)

\displaystyle\tilde{O}_{J}^{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}}\mu^{-1}h_{f,[J]}(\mu t)\overline{D}_{t}\nabla_{a}A_{(f,J)}^{ia}

(57)

We similarly decompose
r_{J}=\bar{r}_{J}+\tilde{r}_{J}
, where

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{J}^{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}}\mu^{-1}h_{f,[J]}(\mu t)A_{(f,J)}^{ij}

(58)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{r}_{J}^{ij}+T^{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}\nabla_{\ell}\bar{r}_{J}^{ij}

\displaystyle={\cal R}^{ij}_{b}\nabla_{\ell}[\nabla_{a}T^{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}r_{J}^{ab}]+{\cal B}^{ij}_{b}[z_{J}^{b},\theta_{\epsilon}]-\tilde{O}_{J}^{ij}

(59)

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{J}^{ij}(\tau k)

\displaystyle=0

(60)

\displaystyle\tilde{O}_{J}^{ij}

\displaystyle=\mu^{-1}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}}h_{f,[J]}(\mu t)\overline{D}_{t}A^{ij}_{(f,J)}.

(61)

Note that clearly
\tilde{w}_{J}=\nabla_{i}\tilde{z}_{J}^{i}=\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\tilde{r}_{J}^{ij}
, as required at the initial time
\tau k
.

3.6
Newton Step summary

The main Proposition in the Newton step, which summarizes all that is needed to proceed to the convex integration step, is given by Proposition
3.1
. Establishing this proposition involves estimating on the one hand explicit parametrices (
\tilde{w}_{J},\tilde{z}_{J},\tilde{r}_{J}
) for the relevant transport equations, and on the other hand remainders
\bar{w}_{J},\bar{z}_{J},\bar{r}_{J}
that solve the remainder transport equations exactly (compare equations (
35
),(
42
), and (
46
) with equations (
50
)-(
59
)). We use the notation

\displaystyle{\bf F}_{J}=\{\bar{w}_{J},\bar{z}_{J},\bar{r}_{J}\}

(62)

to denote the list of tensor fields involved in the main estimate that solve transport type equations for which we require a sharp bound.

Table (
3.7
) states how large all of these tensors should be. The proposition controlling the parametrices as well as the forcing terms
\tilde{O}_{J},\tilde{O}^{i}_{J},\tilde{O}^{ij}_{J}
in the transport equation for the remainders is Proposition
3.3
. The main proposition controlling the terms that solve transport equations (
{\bf F}_{J}
) is Proposition
3.4
, which reduces our desired estimates to applying Grönwall to the appropriate weighted norm. We reduce Proposition
3.4
to the more straightforward Proposition
3.5
by estimating commutator terms. The bounds for
\bar{w}_{J}
begin on line (
105
), those for
\bar{z}_{J}
begin on line (
108
), and those for
r_{J}
, which require the most substantial novelty, begin on line (
109
). The estimates for
u_{J}^{\ell}=T^{\ell}w_{J}
begin on line (
135
) while those for
\rho_{J}^{j\ell}={\cal R}^{j\ell}w_{J}={\cal R}^{j\ell}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}r_{J}^{ab}
begin on line (
140
). These last two estimates rely on the principle that order zero operators cost a log, including when commuting with an advective derivative (Proposition
3.6
). From all these, we prove the claimed estimates for the gluing error
R_{(n+1)}
beginning on line (
143
).

3.7
Newton Step: the estimates

We now recall the estimates we inherit from previous work, where the choice of
\epsilon
was the same but slightly different in notation (
\epsilon=N^{-1/L}\Xi^{-1}=\widehat{N}^{-1}\Xi^{-1}=\widehat{\Xi}^{-1}
). For the coarse scale velocity field and scalar field, we have

[
34
, equation (6.2)]
the following bounds:

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\theta_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}u_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}e_{u}^{1/2},

(63)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}\theta_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}u_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+1-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+1}e_{u}.

(64)

We will refer to derivatives of
(\theta,u,R)
beyond order
L
as “borrowed derivatives”. The idea is that
(\theta,u,R)
come with
L
derivatives, but factors of
\widehat{N}
start to appear in derivatives of higher order when the derivative hits the mollifier. The Newton step will lose three derivatives, so during and after the Newton step, borrowed derivatives start to appear after order
{\underline{L}}\equiv L-3
.

We remark some minor differences regarding (
63
)-(
64
). In
[
34
]
the proof uses the double convolution structure of the mollification, which is also present in our case since Littlewood Paley projections satisfy

P_{\leq q_{\epsilon}}\theta=P_{\leq q_{\epsilon}+1}P_{\leq q_{\epsilon}}\theta=\eta_{\leq q_{\epsilon}+1}\ast\eta_{\leq q_{\epsilon}}\ast\theta.

In
[
34
]
, the parameters
\Xi
and
e_{v}^{1/2}
play the roles of our
\Xi
and
e_{u}^{1/2}
, and the extra loss for borrowed derivatives (
N^{1/L}
in the source) is denoted here by
\widehat{N}
. The bound (
63
) for
|\vec{a}|=0
is not present in
[
34
]
, but is immediate from Definition
2.2
and Minkowski’s inequality.

For the stress, the estimates in
[
35
, equations (628)–(630), equation (661) in the proof of Proposition 18.3, and Proposition 18.6, equation (699)]
give

\displaystyle\|R-R_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1}D_{R}

(65)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}R_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}D_{R}

(66)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}R_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+1-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}D_{R}.

(67)

In the cited source the tensor
R_{\epsilon_{x}}
, stress level
e_{R}
, velocity level
e_{v}^{1/2}
, mollified velocity
v_{\epsilon}
, and material derivative
\overline{D}/\partial t
play the roles of our
R_{\epsilon}
,
D_{R}
,
e_{u}^{1/2}
,
u_{\epsilon}
, and
\overline{D}_{t}
, respectively. The hypotheses used are the stress bounds
\|\nabla^{\vec{a}}R\|_{C^{0}}\leq\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}D_{R}
for
|\vec{a}|\leq L
and
\|\nabla^{\vec{a}}D_{t}R\|_{C^{0}}\leq\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})D_{R}
for
|\vec{a}|\leq L-1
, together with the velocity parts of (
63
)–(
64
). Again we use
\widehat{N}
to denote
N^{1/L}
, which is the notation used in the source.

The terms we need to estimate include not only the scalar fields
\bar{w}_{J}
,
\tilde{w}_{J}
and the fields
z_{J}
and
r_{J}
, but also the fields
u_{J}^{\ell}=T^{\ell}w_{J}
and a trace-free symmetric tensor field
\rho_{J}^{i\ell}
that is defined by

\rho_{J}^{i\ell}={\cal R}^{i\ell}w_{J}={\cal R}^{i\ell}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}r_{J}^{ab}

and whose second order divergence is
w_{J}
(i.e.
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{\ell}\rho^{i\ell}_{J}=w_{J}
). The operator
{\cal R}^{i\ell}
is the order
-2
operator defined in Section
1.1
.

We will associate to each of these tensor fields
F
in our problem a positive number
S_{F}
that is the “size” of
F
. The following table summarizes the sizes of the fields

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{ c|ccccc}F&\bar{w}_{J},w_{J}&\bar{z}_{J}^{i},z_{J}^{i}&\bar{r}_{J}^{ij},r_{J}^{ij}&u_{J}^{\ell}&\rho_{J}^{i\ell}\\
S_{F}&\Xi^{2}\mu^{-1}D_{R,n}&\Xi\mu^{-1}D_{R,n}&\mu^{-1}D_{R,n}&(\log\widehat{\Xi})\Xi^{2}\mu^{-1}D_{R,n}&(\log\widehat{\Xi})\mu^{-1}D_{R,n}\end{array}\right],

Thus
S_{w}=\Xi^{2}\mu^{-1}D_{R,n}
,
S_{z}=\Xi\mu^{-1}D_{R,n}
, etc. For convenience we remind the reader of the choice of
\mu=N^{1/2}\Xi e_{R}^{1/2}=N^{1/2}\Xi^{3/2}D_{R}^{1/2}
from (
16
).

At this point it is instructive to note that most of the
C^{0}
estimates (
\|\bar{w}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim S_{w}
,
\|\bar{z}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim S_{z}
,
\|\tilde{r}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim S_{r}
) are rather straightforward to foresee. For the parametrices
\tilde{w}_{J},\tilde{z}_{J},\tilde{r}_{J}
, they follow by inspecting the defining formulas using
e_{n}(t)\lesssim D_{R,n}
. For
\bar{w}_{J}
and
\bar{z}_{J}
, they come about due to the method of characteristics estimate
\|\bar{w}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim\tau\|\overline{D}_{t}\bar{w}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}
,
\|\bar{z}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim\tau\|\overline{D}_{t}\bar{z}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}
after inspecting the size of the forcing term in the transport equations (note that only three derivatives are taken in these formulas so no “borrowed derivatives” appear at the level of
C^{0}
); the other terms will be treated by Gronwall using the principle that order zero operators cost a log. The most challenging term to estimate is
\bar{r}_{J}
, which requires an involved harmonic analysis of the
{\cal B}
-term in the equation. Once the estimates for
w_{J}
and
r_{J}
are proven, the estimates for
u_{J}
and
\rho_{J}
follow from the general principle that zero-order operators are not bounded on
C^{0}
but rather cost a factor of log, including when commuting with advective derivatives (equation (
101
) and Lemma
3.6
).

We are now ready to estimate the terms in the Newton step. Define

\underline{L}:=L-3,

which will be the new threshold for borrowed derivatives, since formulating the Newton step costs three derivatives. The following is the main result of this section.

Proposition 3.1
.

For all
F\in{\bf F}_{J}\cup\{w_{J},z_{J},r_{J}\}
, we have the estimates

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}F\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}S_{F},

(70)

Moreover, we have the following bounds for
w_{n}
,
u_{n}^{\ell}=T^{\ell}w_{n}
and
\rho_{n}^{j\ell}={\cal R}^{j\ell}w_{n}

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}|\nabla|^{-1/2}w_{n}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}D_{R,n}^{1/2},\qquad 0\leq|\vec{a}|\leq 1

(71)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}u_{n}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}S_{u}

(72)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}^{r}u_{n}\right\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(r+|\vec{a}|-\underline{L})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{r}e_{u}^{1/2},\qquad 0\leq r\leq 1

(73)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}^{r}\rho_{J}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+r-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-r}S_{\rho}\qquad\mbox{on }\mathrm{supp}\,\tilde{\chi}_{k}^{\prime}(t)

(74)

Furthermore there exists a symmetric, trace-free tensor field
R_{(n+1)}
that solves (
38
) and satisfies the bounds for

\displaystyle\|R_{(n+1)}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq D_{R,n+1}

(75)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}R_{(n+1)}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}D_{R,n+1}

(76)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}R_{(n+1)}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+1-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})D_{R,n+1}

(77)

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}_{t}\,(w_{n},R_{(n+1)})

\displaystyle\subseteq J_{n+1}

(78)

Note that (
75
) has implicit constant 1.

We will need the following bounds on the phase functions.

Proposition 3.2
.

For
J=(k,f)
and
|t-k\tau|\leq\tau
, the phase function gradients satisfy

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\nabla\check{\xi}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+1-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}

(79)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}\nabla\check{\xi}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+1-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+1}e_{u}^{1/2}

(80)

These are Proposition 17.3 (equation (558)) and Proposition 17.5 (equation (593)) of
[
35
]
, applied with (
63
)–(
64
). In the source,
v_{\epsilon}
,
\overline{D}/\partial t
,
e_{v}^{1/2}
, and
N^{1/L}
are our
u_{\epsilon}
,
\overline{D}_{t}
,
e_{u}^{1/2}
, and
\widehat{N}
. Thus the source estimates

\|\nabla^{m}\xi\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim_{m}\Xi^{m-1}N^{(m-L)_{+}/L},\qquad\|\nabla^{a}(\overline{D}/\partial t)\nabla\xi\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim_{a}\Xi^{a+1}e_{v}^{1/2}N^{(a+1-L)_{+}/L}

give the two displayed bounds above, on the stated lifespan, after writing
N^{1/L}=\widehat{N}
. Besides the frequency–energy hypotheses in Definition
2.2
, the argument uses the transport equation (
25
), its affine data at
t=k\tau
, and the restriction
|t-k\tau|\leq\tau\lesssim(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}
.

The following weighted norm will be useful.

Definition 3.1
.

The start-weighted norm of a function
F
is

\displaystyle H_{\zeta,M}^{(R)}[F]

\displaystyle=\max_{0\leq r\leq R}\max_{0\leq|\vec{a}|+r\leq M}\frac{\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}^{r}F\|_{C^{0}}}{\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+1-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\zeta^{r}}

(81)

Note that
R\in\{0,1\}
is a number, not to be confused with the stress tensor.

When we run into terms that involve a mix of spatial and advective derivatives, the following Lemma is useful. This lemma will be applied to
\tilde{O}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

For any multi-indices
\vec{a},\vec{b}
such that
|\vec{a}|+|\vec{b}|\leq M
and for
\zeta\geq\Xi e_{u}^{1/2}
we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}\nabla_{\vec{b}}F\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+|\vec{b}|+1-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+|\vec{b}|}\zeta H_{\zeta,M}^{(1)}[F]

(82)

Proof.

Let
M
be given. We proceed by induction on
|\vec{b}|\leq M
. The case
|\vec{b}|=0
follows directly from the definition of
H_{\zeta,M}^{(1)}[F]
. Now assume the bound holds for
|\vec{b}|-1
, and write
\nabla_{\vec{b}}=\nabla_{b_{1}}\nabla_{\check{b}}
where
|\check{b}|=|\vec{b}|-1
. We have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}\nabla_{\vec{b}}F

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\vec{a}}\nabla_{b_{1}}\overline{D}_{t}\nabla_{\check{b}}F-\nabla_{\vec{a}}[\nabla_{b_{1}}u_{\epsilon}^{i}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{\check{b}}F]

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}\nabla_{\vec{b}}F\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+|\vec{b}|+1-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+|\vec{b}|}\zeta H_{\zeta,M}^{(1)}[F]

\displaystyle+\overset{\sim}{\sum}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}\nabla_{b_{1}}u_{\epsilon}^{i}\|_{C^{0}}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{2}}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{\check{b}}F\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+|\vec{b}|+1-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+|\vec{b}|}\zeta H_{\zeta,M}^{(1)}[F]

\displaystyle+\overset{\sim}{\sum}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}_{1}|+1-L)_{+}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}_{2}|+1+|\check{b}|-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+|\vec{b}|}\Xi e_{u}^{1/2}H_{\zeta,M}^{(1)}[F]

\displaystyle\lesssim\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+|\vec{b}|+1-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+|\vec{b}|}\zeta H_{\zeta,M}^{(1)}[F]

∎

We also have a chain rule for the weighted norm.

Lemma 3.2
.

K
be a compact neighborhood of the image of
(\check{R}=R_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n}),\nabla\xi_{k})
and let
G
be
C^{\infty}
on a neighborhood of
K
. Then

\displaystyle H_{\Xi e_{u}^{1/2},M}^{(R)}[G(\check{R},\nabla\xi_{k})]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,K,G}1

(83)

Proof.

By the chain and product rules we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}^{r}G(\check{R},\nabla\xi_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{|\vec{a}|+R}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\partial^{m}G\prod_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{i}}\overline{D}_{t}^{r_{i}}\check{R}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{m_{2}}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{j}}\overline{D}_{t}^{r_{j}}\nabla\xi_{k}

where the sum ranges over indices such that
\sum|\vec{a}_{i}|+\sum|\vec{a}_{j}|=|\vec{a}|
and
\sum_{i}r_{i}+\sum_{j}r_{j}=r
and the empty product is
1
. Hence,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}^{r}G(\check{R},\nabla\xi_{k})\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{|\vec{a}|+R}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\prod_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}_{i}|+r_{i}-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}_{i}|}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{r_{i}}\cdot

\displaystyle\,\,\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{m_{2}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}_{j}|+1-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}_{j}|}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{r_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{|\vec{a}|+R}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{r}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\widehat{N}^{(\sum_{i}(|\vec{a}_{i}|+r_{i})-L)_{+}}\widehat{N}^{(\sum_{j}|\vec{a}_{j}|+1-L)_{+}}

where the last line we used the counting inequality with
z=L
and
z=L-1
. The proof now follows from

\displaystyle(\sum_{i}(|\vec{a}_{i}|+r_{i})-L)_{+}+(\sum_{j}|\vec{a}_{j}|+1-L)_{+}\leq

\displaystyle\leq(\sum_{i}|\vec{a}_{i}|+1-L)_{+}+(\sum_{j}|\vec{a}_{j}|+1-L)_{+}\leq(|\vec{a}|+1-L)_{+}

∎

Corollary 3.1
.

\displaystyle H_{\tau^{-1},M}^{(1)}[A_{(f,J)}^{j\ell}]

\displaystyle\lesssim D_{R,n}

(84)

Proof.

This follows quickly from the previous Lemmas applied to (
34
). Note that the cost of an advective derivative is
\tau^{-1}
rather than
(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})
due to the factors of
\chi_{k}^{2}
and
e_{n}(t)
.
∎

The following proposition summarizes the bounds on terms that do not solve a transport equation

Proposition 3.3
.

For all
\vec{a}
, we have the bounds

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{w}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}+\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{w}_{J}\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}S_{w}

(85)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{z}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}+\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{z}_{J}\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}S_{z}

(86)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{r}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}+\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{r}_{J}\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}S_{r}

(87)

and

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{O}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}+\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{O}_{J}\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-1}S_{w}

(88)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{O}_{J}^{i}\|_{C^{0}}++\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{O}_{J}^{i}\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-1}S_{z}

(89)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{O}_{J}^{ij}\|_{C^{0}}+\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{O}_{J}^{ij}\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-1}S_{r}

(90)

Proof.

It suffices to prove the bounds for the
C^{0}
norms since they imply the bounds on the
\dot{C}^{\alpha}
norms by interpolation. We only prove the bounds for
\tilde{w}_{J}
and
\tilde{O}_{J}
since the other bounds will then be similar.
The bound for
\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{w}_{J}\|_{0}
follows from (
76
).

The bound for
\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{O}_{J}\|_{0}
follows by taking
\nabla_{\vec{b}}=\operatorname{div}\operatorname{div}
(thus
|\vec{b}|=2
) and
F=A_{J}
in Lemma
3.1
. This choice yields

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{O}_{J}\|_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mu^{-1}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+3-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+2}\tau^{-1}H_{\tau^{-1},M}^{(1)}[A_{J}],

\displaystyle\lesssim\mu^{-1}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+2}\tau^{-1}D_{R,n}

\displaystyle=\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-1}S_{w}

which is the desired bound. ∎

Fix some
3
3

3

The value of
\alpha
is unimportant – the point is the well-posedness of the equations holds in
C^{k,\alpha}
spaces but not in
C^{k}
spaces.

\alpha\in(0,1)
. The proof of Proposition
3.1
relies on the following weighted norm:

\displaystyle\mathpzc{h}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{F\in{\bf F}_{J}}\sum_{|\vec{a}|\leq L^{\prime}}(S_{F}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|})^{-1}\left(\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}F\|_{C^{0}}+\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}F\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}\right).

(91)

Here and in what follows we suppress the dependence of
\mathpzc{h}(t)
on the index
J
and on
L^{\prime}
. We write
\mathpzc{h}_{L^{\prime}}
to emphasize dependence on
L^{\prime}
. Notice that
\mathpzc{h}(t)
vanishes at the initial time
t_{J}=t_{(k,n)}=k\tau
. In the following analysis, we simplify notation by assuming the initial time is
t_{J}=0
.

Proposition 3.4

(Main proposition in the Newton step)
.

We have the estimate

\displaystyle\mathpzc{h}(t)

\displaystyle\leq C(\log\widehat{\Xi})\Xi e_{u}^{1/2}\int_{[0,t]}(1+\mathpzc{h}(s))ds.

(92)

for some
C>0
independent of the frequency energy levels
(\Xi,D_{u},D_{R})
and independent of
N
, but
C
is allowed to depend on the step
n
of the Newton iteration and on
L^{\prime}
, the order of differentiation that
\mathpzc{h}
controls.

In particular, by Gronwall,
\mathpzc{h}(t)\lesssim 1
for
|t|\leq\tau
.

Recall the notation
\hat{q}
for the integer satisfying
\widehat{\Xi}\sim 2^{\hat{q}}
. The following criterion will be useful for bounding
\mathpzc{h}(t)

Lemma 3.3
.

For any function
f\in C(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and any multi-index
\vec{a}
we have that

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}f\|_{C^{0}}+\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}f\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}f\|_{C^{0}}+\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\sup_{q>\hat{q}}2^{\alpha q}\|P_{q}\nabla_{\vec{a}}f\|_{C^{0}}

(93)

(In fact, the two sides are equivalent up to constants.)

Indeed, this lemma follows quickly from the following standard Littlewood-Paley characterization of
\dot{C}^{\alpha}
seminorm,

\|f\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}\sim\sup_{q}2^{\alpha q}\|P_{q}f\|_{C^{0}},

which is valid for continuous
f
. (A proof can be found in
[
37
, Proposition 9.1 and Appendix A, proof of Proposition 9.1]
.)

We will also use the following Lemma about commuting spatial derivatives and Littlewood-Paley projections with the advective derivative. We define

\|f(t)\|_{C^{0}}:=\sup_{x}|f(t,x)|.

Lemma 3.4
.

For any
F\in{\bf F}_{J}
and any multi-index
\vec{a}
of order
|\vec{a}|\leq L^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}F(t)\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|\overline{D}_{t}\nabla_{\vec{a}}F(s)\|_{C^{0}}ds

(94)

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{t}\nabla_{\vec{a}}F(s)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}F(s)+{O}\left(\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+1}e_{u}^{1/2}S_{F}\mathpzc{h}(s)\right),

(95)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\overline{D}_{t}\nabla_{\vec{a}}P_{q}F(s)&=P_{q}\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}F(s)\\
&+\min\{1,2^{-\alpha q}\widehat{\Xi}^{\alpha}\}\,{O}\left(\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+1}e_{u}^{1/2}S_{F}\mathpzc{h}(s)\right)\end{split}

(96)

Proof.

Inequality (
94
) is a consequence of the method of characteristics. For the second estimate, we compute

\displaystyle[\nabla_{\vec{a}},\overline{D}_{t}]F

\displaystyle=\overset{\sim}{\sum_{\vec{a}_{1},\vec{a}_{2}}}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}u_{\epsilon}^{i}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{2}}\nabla_{i}F\,1_{|\vec{a}_{1}|\geq 1}

\displaystyle\|[\nabla_{\vec{a}},\overline{D}_{t}]F\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\overset{\sim}{\sum_{\vec{a}_{1},\vec{a}_{2}}}[\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}_{1}|-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}_{1}|}e_{u}^{1/2}][\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}_{2}|+1-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}_{2}|}S_{F}]

where the sum ranges over multi-indices with
|\vec{a}_{1}|+|\vec{a}_{2}|=|\vec{a}|
. Now employ the counting inequality with
(|\vec{a}_{1}|-1,|\vec{a}_{2}|+1,{\underline{L}}-1)
, all
\geq 0
, to get

(|\vec{a}_{1}|-L)_{+}+(|\vec{a}_{2}|+1-{\underline{L}})_{+}\leq(|\vec{a}_{1}|-1+1-{\underline{L}})_{+}+(|\vec{a}_{2}|+1-{\underline{L}})_{+}\leq(|\vec{a}_{1}|-1+|\vec{a}_{2}|+1-{\underline{L}})_{+}=(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}

Inequality (
95
) follows.

The last bound in this lemma is a special cases of Lemma
3.6
below where we take
{\cal Q}
to be the identity map.
∎

Based on Lemmas
3.3
and
3.4
, the proof of the Main Proposition (Proposition
3.4
) reduces to the following

Proposition 3.5
.

For any
F\in{\bf F}_{J}
we have for all
|\vec{a}|\leq L^{\prime}
and all
q>\hat{q}
the bounds

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}F(s)\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-1}S_{F}(1+\mathpzc{h}(t))

(97)

\displaystyle\|P_{q}\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}F(s)\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim 2^{-\alpha q}\widehat{\Xi}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-1}S_{F}(1+\mathpzc{h}(t))

(98)

The following standard spatial derivative bounds will be used.

Proposition 3.6
.

If
{\cal Q}
is a convolution operator whose symbol is degree
0
homogeneous and smooth away from
0
then

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}P_{q}{\cal Q}F\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-\alpha q}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}F\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

(99)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}{\cal Q}F\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}F\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

(100)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}{\cal Q}F\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\log\widehat{\Xi})\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}F\|_{C^{0}}+\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}F\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

(101)

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

Let
\eta_{q}(h)=2^{dq}\eta_{0}(2^{q}h)
be the convolution kernel representing
P_{q}{\cal Q}
. Then
\eta_{q}
has integral
0
and (
99
) follows from

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{a}}P_{q}{\cal Q}F

\displaystyle=\int(\nabla_{\vec{a}}F(x+h)-\nabla_{\vec{a}}F(x))\eta_{q}(h)dh

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\vec{a}}P_{q}{\cal Q}F|

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}F\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}\int|h|^{\alpha}|\eta_{q}(h)|dh.

The second bound follows from the first one and the Littlewood Paley characterization of Hölder spaces. The third estimate is obtained by summing

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}{\cal Q}F\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{q=0}^{\hat{q}}\|P_{q}{\cal Q}\nabla_{\vec{a}}F\|_{C^{0}}+\sum_{q=\hat{q}}^{\infty}2^{-\alpha q}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}F\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

using the fact that
\|P_{q}{\cal Q}\|\lesssim 1
as an operator on
C^{0}
.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

For
|\vec{a}|\leq L^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}u_{J}\|_{C^{0}}+\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}u_{J}\|_{\dot{C^{\alpha}}}\lesssim\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-L)_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}S_{u}(1+\mathpzc{h}).

(102)

Proof.

We use Proposition
3.6
with
{\cal Q}=T
and
F=w_{J}
. This result gives for
|\vec{a}|\leq L^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}u_{J}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle=\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}Tw_{J}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim(\log\widehat{\Xi})\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}w_{J}\|_{C^{0}}+\widehat{\Xi}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}w_{J}\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}.

This is in turn bounded by

\displaystyle\lesssim(\log\widehat{\Xi})\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}S_{w}(1+\mathpzc{h})+\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}S_{w}(1+\mathpzc{h})

\displaystyle\lesssim\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}S_{u}(1+\mathpzc{h})

where we have used the definition of
\mathpzc{h}=\mathpzc{h}_{L^{\prime}}
.

For the
\dot{C}^{\alpha}
bound, we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}u_{J}\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}w_{J}\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\widehat{\Xi}^{\alpha}S_{w}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}(\mathpzc{h}+1)

(103)

where the first inequality follows by Proposition
3.6
and the second inequality follows from the definition of
\mathpzc{h}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.5
for
\bar{w}_{J}
.

For the
C^{0}
bound on
\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}\bar{w}_{J}
, we use the equation (
51
):

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}\bar{w}_{J}\|_{C^{0}}
