\displaystyle\leq(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})\int_{t(I)}^{t}\|\nabla\check{\xi}(s)-\nabla\xi_{I}(s)\|_{0}ds+\tau(\|\nabla(T^{j}w\nabla_{j}\xi_{J})\|_{C^{0}})

\displaystyle\|\nabla\check{\xi}(t)-\nabla\xi_{I}(t)\|_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{C\Xi e_{u}^{1/2}\tau}\tau\Xi S_{u}\lesssim N^{-1/2}(\mathrm{D}_{u}/\mathrm{D}_{R})^{-1/2}.

(198)

In particular, since
N\geq\hat{C}
by (
7
), we have that
|\nabla\xi_{I}-\nabla\check{\xi}_{I}|\lesssim\widehat{C}^{-1/2}
. For all
\widehat{C}
large enough,
\nabla\xi_{I}
take values in the domain of the functions
\gamma_{f}
and
B^{j\ell}
, so that the construction of the convex integration wave
\Theta
will be well-defined, and also satisfies
|\nabla\xi_{I}-f|\leq|f|/5
, so that
\lambda\nabla\xi_{I}
takes values in the region where the multiplier for
P_{I}
is equal to
1
.

We obtain the following bounds on the amplitudes
\gamma_{I}
defined in (
172
).

Proposition 4.5

(Amplitude bounds)
.

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}^{r}\gamma_{I}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+1-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-r}D_{R,n}^{1/2}\,\qquad 0\leq r\leq 1,

(199)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}^{2}\gamma_{I}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+2-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\epsilon_{t}^{-1}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})D_{R,n}^{1/2}.

(200)

Proof.

Put
\check{R}_{n}=\tilde{R}_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n})
and apply the weighted chain and product estimates to

\gamma_{(f,k,n)}=\chi_{k}e_{n}^{1/2}(t)\gamma_{f}(\check{R}_{n},\nabla\xi_{k}).

For the stress and phase gradients, the advective derivative costs
\Xi e_{u}^{1/2}
by Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
, whereas differentiating
\chi_{k}
or
e_{n}^{1/2}
costs
\tau^{-1}
. Equivalently, we may use the standard estimates from
[
35
]
with the source velocity level
e_{v}^{1/2}
replaced by
\widetilde{e}_{u}^{1/2}
from (
15
), since
\Xi\widetilde{e}_{u}^{1/2}\sim_{L,\eta}\tau^{-1}
. This proves (
199
).

For two material derivatives, Proposition
4.3
shows that taking two advective derivatives of
\check{R}_{n}
costs
\epsilon_{t}^{-1}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})
. When two advective derivatives fall on
\chi_{k}
,
e_{n}^{1/2}(t)
or
\nabla\xi_{k}
, the cost is at most
\tau^{-2}
, and

\displaystyle\tau^{-2}\lesssim_{L,\eta}\epsilon_{t}^{-1}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2}).

(201)

by (
14
), (
17
), and the lower bound (
7
), which yields the
|\vec{a}|=0
case of (
200
). Upon taking
|\vec{a}|
spatial derivatives, the worst case is that a factor of
\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+2-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}
may appear when all spatial derivatives land on
\tilde{D}_{t}^{2}\nabla\xi_{k}
(see (
195
)), which yields the claimed spatial derivative bound in (
200
).
∎

The coarser versions with
D_{R}^{1/2}
in place of
D_{R,n}^{1/2}
follow immediately.

Having estimated the phase functions, we apply the Microlocal Lemma
[
34
, Lemma 4.1]
, which states that convolution operators such as
P_{I}=K_{I}\ast
and
T^{\ell}P_{I}=K_{I}^{\ell}\ast
that are localized to frequency
\lambda
behave to leading order like multiplication operators with an explicit error. The kernels here are Schwartz and defined by scaling

K_{I}(h)=\lambda^{d}K_{f(I)}(\lambda h)
and
K_{I}^{\ell}(h)=\lambda^{d}K_{f(I)}^{\ell}(\lambda h)
and therefore inherit natural moment bounds
\||h|^{k}K_{I}\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim_{k}\lambda^{-k}
. We have
\widehat{K}_{I}(\lambda\nabla\xi_{I})=1
and
\widehat{K}_{I}^{\ell}(\lambda\nabla\xi_{I})=m^{\ell}(\lambda\nabla\xi_{I})=m^{\ell}(\nabla\xi_{I})

where the last equality uses the degree-zero homogeneity of
m
. Consequently

\displaystyle\Theta_{I}

\displaystyle=g_{[I]}(\mu t)e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}(\theta_{I}+\delta\theta_{I})

(202)

\displaystyle T^{\ell}\Theta_{I}

\displaystyle=g_{[I]}(\mu t)e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}(u_{I}^{\ell}+\delta u_{I}^{\ell})

(203)

\displaystyle u_{I}^{\ell}

\displaystyle=m^{\ell}(\nabla\xi_{I})\theta_{I}

(204)

We state estimates for
\delta\theta_{I}
and
\delta u_{I}^{\ell}
. The upshot of
[
34
, Lemma 7.5]
is that, for the sake of estimates in
C^{k}
spaces, one has the heuristic

\displaystyle\delta\theta_{I}\sim\delta u_{I}

\displaystyle\sim\lambda^{-1}(\nabla\theta_{I}+\theta_{I}\nabla^{2}\xi_{I}).

(205)

(See
[
34
, Equations (7.21)-(7.22)]
for a more precise formula.) Hence, for
r=0,1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}^{r}\delta\theta_{I}\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}^{r}\delta u_{I}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{1/2}N^{-1}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+2-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-r}D_{R,n}^{1/2},

(206)

The inputs for these bounds are the phase, principal-amplitude, coarse-velocity, and scaled-kernel moment bounds, which are supplied here by Propositions
4.4
and
4.5
, (
176
), and the preceding kernel estimates. For comparison to the analogous estimates of
[
34
, Lemma 7.5]
: the gain of
B_{\lambda}^{-1}N^{-1}=\Xi/\lambda
in the source is replaced here by
N^{-1}=\Xi/\lambda
; the number of derivatives without loss here is
{\underline{L}}
in place of
L
; the loss for borrowed derivatives is
\widehat{N}=N^{1/L}
in place of
N^{1/L}
; the cost of an advective derivative is
\Xi\widetilde{e}_{u}^{1/2}=\tau^{-1}
in place of the anomalous loss of
\tau^{-1}
in
[
34
]
; the amplitude of
\theta_{I}=(2\lambda)^{1/2}\gamma_{I}
is
\lambda^{1/2}D_{R,n}^{1/2}
in place of
e_{R}^{1/2}
.
The same heuristic (
205
) leads to our bounds on the second advective derivative:

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}^{2}\delta\theta_{I}\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}^{2}\delta u_{I}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{1/2}N^{-1}\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+3-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\epsilon_{t}^{-1}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})D_{R,n}^{1/2}.

(207)

Intuitively, as in
[
34
, Lemma 7.5]
we take two advective derivatives of
[
34
, Equations (7.21)-(7.22)]
, which supply more complicated but rigorous versions of (
205
), and we apply the the local jet and coarse-velocity bounds (
176
)–(
178
) and Propositions
4.4
and
4.5
. The cost of
\epsilon_{t}^{-1}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})
ultimately arises from Proposition
4.5
when two advective derivatives hit the
\gamma_{I}
, which dominates all the other terms by inequality (
201
). The factor of
\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+3-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}
arises from Proposition
4.4
in the worst case when one sees a term of the form
\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}^{2}\nabla^{2}\xi_{I}
.

5
Estimating the corrections

Here we gather estimates for the corrections

\displaystyle\Theta_{I}

\displaystyle=g_{[I]}(\mu t)P_{I}[e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}(2\lambda)^{1/2}\gamma_{I}]

(208)

\displaystyle T^{\ell}\Theta_{I}

\displaystyle=g_{[I]}(\mu t)T^{\ell}P_{I}[e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}(2\lambda)^{1/2}\gamma_{I}]

(209)

Since
g_{[I]}
has size
1
and both
\|P_{I}\|\lesssim 1
and
\|T^{\ell}P_{I}\|\lesssim 1
, we immediately obtain from (
199
) that

\displaystyle\|\Theta_{I}\|_{C^{0}}+\|T^{\ell}\Theta_{I}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{1/2}\|\gamma_{I}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim\lambda^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}

(210)

Since
P_{I}=P_{\approx\lambda}P_{I}
and
T^{\ell}P_{I}=P_{\approx\lambda}T^{\ell}P_{I}
both localize to frequency
\lambda
, this bound implies

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\Theta_{I}\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}T^{\ell}\Theta_{I}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\lambda^{|\vec{a}|+1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}

(211)

Writing
|\nabla|^{-1/2}P_{I}=[|\nabla|^{-1/2}P_{\approx\lambda}]P_{I}
, where
\||\nabla|^{-1/2}P_{\approx\lambda}\|\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}
, we also obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}|\nabla|^{-1/2}\Theta_{I}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}\lambda^{|\vec{a}|}D_{R}^{1/2},

(212)

which finishes the verification of the claim (
10
).

Now define the vector field
\accentset{\ast}{u}^{\ell}=\tilde{u}^{\ell}+(u^{\ell}-u_{\epsilon}^{\ell})+T^{\ell}\Theta
and the associated advective derivative

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{D}_{t}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}+\accentset{\ast}{u}\cdot\nabla

(213)

Definition 5.1
.

For
\mathring{D}_{t}\in\{\partial_{t},\overline{D}_{t},\tilde{D}_{t},\accentset{\ast}{D}_{t}\}
define the final weighted norm

\displaystyle\mathring{H}^{*}[F]

\displaystyle=\max_{0\leq r\leq 1}\max_{0\leq|\vec{a}|+r\leq L}\frac{\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\mathring{D}_{t}^{r}F\|_{C^{0}}}{(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}}

(214)

where we recall
\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}=(N\Xi)^{3/2}D_{R}^{1/2}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

The final weighted norms are comparable up to implicit constants

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[F]\lesssim H^{*}[F]\lesssim\bar{H}^{*}[F]\lesssim\tilde{H}^{*}[F]\lesssim\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[F]

(215)

Furthermore there is a product rule
\mathring{H}^{*}[FG]\lesssim\mathring{H}^{*}[F]\mathring{H}^{*}[G]
.

Proof.

Since all the inequalities are proven similarly, we only give the proof of
\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[F]\lesssim H^{*}[F]
, which contains all the needed ideas. We have, for
0\leq|\vec{a}|\leq L-1
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{a}}\accentset{\ast}{D}_{t}F=\nabla_{\vec{a}}\partial_{t}F+\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\tilde{u}^{i}\nabla_{i}F)+\nabla_{\vec{a}}[(u^{i}-u_{\epsilon}^{i})\nabla_{i}F]+\nabla_{\vec{a}}[T^{i}\Theta\nabla_{i}F]

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\accentset{\ast}{D}_{t}F\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\partial_{t}F\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\overset{\sim}{\sum}(\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}\tilde{u}\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}(u-u_{\epsilon})\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}T\Theta\|_{C^{0}})\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{2}}\nabla F\|_{C^{0}}1_{|\vec{a}_{2}|<|\vec{a}_{1}|}

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}H^{*}[F]+\overset{\sim}{\sum}(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}_{1}|}\left(e_{u}^{1/2}+\frac{e_{u}^{1/2}}{N}+(N\Xi)^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}\right)(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}_{2}|+1}H^{*}[F]

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}H^{*}[F]

In the last line we used (
178
), Lemma
4.1
, Lemma
3.7
and (
211
).
∎

We obtain the following estimate for the new velocity field

Proposition 5.2
.

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[\accentset{\ast}{u}]

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}

(216)

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[\accentset{\ast}{\theta}]

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}

(217)

Proof.

We have
\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[\accentset{\ast}{u}]\leq\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[\tilde{u}]++\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[u-u_{\epsilon}]+\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[T[\Theta]]
. By Proposition
5.1
it suffices to bound

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}^{r}\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{r}e_{u}^{1/2}

(218)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\partial_{t}^{r}(u-u_{\epsilon})\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}(e_{u}^{1/2}/N)

(219)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\partial_{t}^{r}T[\Theta]\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}(N\Xi)^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}

(220)

since the right hand side of each of these inequalities is bounded by the right hand side of (
220
), which is our goal estimate. The first of these bounds follows from (
178
), the second from Lemma
3.7
, and the third from the following calculation, which establishes the case
|\vec{a}|=0
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}T^{\ell}[\theta]

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}T^{\ell}P_{I}[g_{[I]}(\mu t)e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}\theta_{I}]

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}T[\theta]\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\overset{\sim}{\sum}\mu^{r_{1}}[\lambda\|\partial_{t}\xi_{I}\|_{C^{0}}]^{r_{2}}\|\partial_{t}^{r_{3}}\theta_{I}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:transportPhase}}}{{\lesssim}}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\mu^{r_{1}}[\lambda\|\tilde{u}\|_{C^{0}}\|\nabla\xi_{I}\|_{C^{0}}]^{r_{2}}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{3}}H^{*}[\theta_{I}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\overset{\sim}{\sum}\mu^{r_{1}}[\lambda\|\tilde{u}\|_{C^{0}}\|\nabla\xi_{I}\|_{C^{0}}]^{r_{2}}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{3}}H^{*}[\theta_{I}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:tildeuepbd1},\eqref{eq:phaseGrad},\eqref{eq:comparable}}}{{\lesssim}}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\mu^{r_{1}}[(N\Xi)(\Xi D_{u})^{1/2}]^{r_{2}}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{3}}\tilde{H}^{*}[\theta_{I}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:NlowerBd}}}{{\lesssim}}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{1}}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{2}}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{3}}\tilde{H}^{*}[\theta_{I}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}\tilde{H}^{*}[\theta_{I}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:firstAdvecAmplitudes}}}{{\lesssim}}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}\lambda^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}

Our desired bound on
\nabla_{\vec{a}}\partial_{t}T[\Theta]
follows from the fact that the operator
TP_{I}=P_{\approx\lambda}TP_{I}
localizes to frequency
\lambda
.

The bounds for
\accentset{\ast}{\theta}
follow the same argument, but are easier as the operator
T
is not involved.
∎

6
The error terms in the convex integration step

Recall that prior to the convex integration we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta_{\Gamma}+T^{j}\theta_{\Gamma}\nabla_{j}\theta_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}[S_{(\Gamma)}^{j\ell}+P_{(\Gamma)}^{j\ell}+R_{(\Gamma)}^{j\ell}]

(221)

\displaystyle S_{(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{I=(f,k,n)\in\mathcal{I}_{+}}g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)e_{n}(t)\chi_{k}^{2}\gamma_{f}^{2}\left(\check{p}_{I}\right)B^{j\ell}(\nabla\check{\xi}_{k})

(222)

The term
P_{(\Gamma)}
is the “acceptable” error from the Newton steps.

When we construct
\accentset{\ast}{\theta}=\theta_{\Gamma}+\Theta
, we get the following error terms

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\accentset{\ast}{\theta}+\accentset{\ast}{u}^{j}\nabla_{j}\accentset{\ast}{\theta}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}\accentset{\ast}{R}^{j\ell}

(223)

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{R}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=R_{T}^{j\ell}+R_{H}^{j\ell}+R_{M}^{j\ell}+R_{S}^{j\ell}+P_{(\Gamma)}^{j\ell}+R_{(\Gamma)}^{j\ell}

(224)

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}R_{T}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\Theta+\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}^{a}\nabla_{a}\Theta+T^{a}\Theta\nabla_{a}\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon}

(225)

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}R_{H}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}}T^{a}\Theta_{I}\nabla_{a}\Theta_{I}

(226)

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}R_{M}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=T^{j}[(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})]\nabla_{j}\Theta+T^{j}\Theta\nabla_{j}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})

(227)

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}R_{S}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{+}}\left\{T^{j}\Theta_{I}\nabla_{j}\Theta_{\bar{I}}+T^{j}\Theta_{\bar{I}}\nabla_{j}\Theta_{I}-\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}[g_{[I]}^{2}e_{n}(t)\chi_{k}^{2}\gamma_{f}^{2}(\check{p}_{I})B^{j\ell}(\nabla\check{\xi}_{I})]\right\}

(228)

Note that there are no terms where
\Theta_{I}
interacts with
\Theta_{J}
for
J\notin\{I,\bar{I}\}
. This is the case thanks to (
175
). The fact that self-interaction terms such as (
226
) are well-controlled was first observed in
[
34
]
.

The term
R_{S}
is the “flow error”. Using the divergence form principle of Section
A.2
, we can write

\displaystyle T^{j}\Theta_{I}\nabla_{j}\Theta_{\bar{I}}+T^{j}\Theta_{\bar{I}}\nabla_{j}\Theta_{I}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}[T^{j}\Theta_{I}\Theta_{\bar{I}}+T^{j}\Theta_{\bar{I}}\Theta_{I}]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}K_{\lambda}^{j\ell}\ast[\Theta_{I},\Theta_{\bar{I}}]

where
K_{\lambda}^{j\ell}
is a specific trace free kernel.
Since only its double divergence is used, replacing
K_{\lambda}^{j\ell}
by its symmetric part does not change the preceding identity.

Let
K_{\lambda,\mathrm{sym}}
denote this symmetric part, constructed as in
[
31
, Section 4.5.2, equations (15)–(17)]
with a fixed common annular cutoff chosen to equal one on the supports of the symbols of
P_{I}
and
P_{\bar{I}}
. The same scaling gives the moment bound in equation (19) there. After factoring out
g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)
, the present pair has the form of the conjugate corrections in
[
31
, Lemma 4.2]
, and that lemma gives

\displaystyle K_{\lambda,\mathrm{sym}}^{j\ell}\ast[\Theta_{I},\Theta_{\bar{I}}]

\displaystyle=g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)\left[\widehat{K}_{\lambda,\mathrm{sym}}^{j\ell}(\lambda\nabla\xi_{I},-\lambda\nabla\xi_{I})|\theta_{I}|^{2}+\delta B_{I}^{j\ell}\right]

(229)

where
\delta B_{I}^{j\ell}
denotes the symmetrized remainder. By
[
31
, equation (20)]
, equivalently (
320
), and the degree-zero homogeneity of the SQG multiplier, we have
\widehat{K}_{\lambda,\mathrm{sym}}^{j\ell}(\lambda p,-\lambda p)=(2\lambda)^{-1}B^{j\ell}(p)
whenever
\lambda p
and
-\lambda p
lie in the corresponding cutoff plateaux where the symbol of
P_{I}
and
P_{\bar{I}}
are equal to
1
; the preceding phase estimate verifies this for
p=\nabla\xi_{I}
. Here

\displaystyle B^{j\ell}(p)

\displaystyle=-i(\nabla^{j}m^{\ell}(p)+\nabla^{\ell}m^{j}(p)),

and
m^{\ell}(p)=i\epsilon^{\ell a}p_{a}|p|^{-1}
is the SQG multiplier. Since
\theta_{I}
is real, the factor
(2\lambda)^{-1}
is exactly canceled by
|\theta_{I}|^{2}=2\lambda e_{n}\chi_{k}^{2}\gamma_{f}^{2}(p_{I})
. Thus one representative
I\in\mathcal{I}_{+}
contributes the full prepared tensor, and we arrive at the following expression for the conjugate interactions:

\displaystyle T^{j}\Theta_{I}\nabla_{j}\Theta_{\bar{I}}+T^{j}\Theta_{\bar{I}}\nabla_{j}\Theta_{I}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}\left[g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)\left[e_{n}(t)\chi_{k}^{2}\gamma_{f}^{2}(p_{I})B^{j\ell}(\nabla\xi_{I})+\delta B_{I}^{j\ell}\right]\right],

(230)

where
\delta B_{I}^{j\ell}
is the remainder furnished by that lemma. Proposition 4.5 of
[
31
]
applies to this same kernel and estimates the remainder. We can then write the
R_{S}
term as

\displaystyle R_{S}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{+}}g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)\left\{e_{n}(t)\chi_{k}^{2}\left[\gamma_{f}^{2}(p_{I})B^{j\ell}(\nabla\xi_{I}){-}\gamma_{f}^{2}(\check{p}_{I})B^{j\ell}(\nabla\check{\xi}_{I})\right]+\delta B_{I}^{j\ell}\right\}

(231)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{+}}(R_{SI}^{j\ell}+g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)\delta B_{I}^{j\ell}).

(232)

We bound this error using (
198
) and our other estimates for the construction components.

7
Estimating
R_{S}

We now begin our estimates on the stress errors. We rely the following propositions:

Proposition 7.1

(Chain rule)
.

Let
\mathring{D}_{t}\in\{\partial_{t},\overline{D}_{t},\tilde{D}_{t},\accentset{\ast}{D}_{t}\}
and
F
be smooth. Let
G
be a
C^{\infty}
function defined on a compact neighborhood of the image of
F
. Then

\displaystyle\mathring{H}^{*}[G(F)]

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\mathring{H}^{*}[F])^{L}

(233)

Proof.

We compute for
0\leq r\leq 1
,
0\leq r+|\vec{a}|\leq L

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{a}}\mathring{D}_{t}^{r}G(F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{|\vec{a}|+r}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\partial^{k}G(F)\prod_{i=0}^{k}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{i}}\mathring{D}_{t}^{r_{i}}F

where the sum is over appropriate indices such that
\sum_{i}|\vec{a}_{i}|=|\vec{a}|
and
\sum_{i}r_{i}=r
. Then

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\mathring{D}_{t}^{r}G(F)\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{|\vec{a}|+r}\|\partial^{k}G\|_{C^{0}}\prod_{i=0}^{k}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{i}}\mathring{D}_{t}^{r_{i}}F\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{|\vec{a}|+r}\prod_{i=0}^{k}\left[(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}_{i}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{i}}\mathring{H}[F]\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}(1+\mathring{H}[F])^{|\vec{a}|+r},

which is the desired bound.
∎

For the following proposition, recall that there exists a ball of radius
K
about
(0,(1,2),(2,1))
such that the range of
(R_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n}),\nabla\check{\xi}_{I})
and also the range of
(\tilde{R}_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n}),\nabla\xi_{I})
are guaranteed to lie in this ball.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
G
be a
C^{\infty}
function defined on the closed ball of radius
K
about
(0,(1,2),(2,1))
. Then

\displaystyle\bar{H}^{*}[G(R_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n}),\nabla\check{\xi}_{I})-G(\tilde{R}_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n}),\nabla\xi_{I})]

\displaystyle\lesssim(D_{u}/D_{R})^{-1/2}N^{-1/2}

(234)

Proof.

The
C^{0}
bound is given by the mean value theorem and (
187
) and (
198
):

\displaystyle\|G(R_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n}),\nabla\check{\xi}_{I})

\displaystyle-G(\tilde{R}_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n}),\nabla\check{\xi}_{I})\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla G\|_{C^{0}}\left(\frac{\|R_{(n)}-\tilde{R}_{(n)}\|_{C^{0}}}{M_{e}D_{R,n}}+\|\nabla\xi_{k}-\nabla\check{\xi}_{k}\|_{C^{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim(D_{u}/D_{R})^{-1/2}N^{-1/2}.

For
1\leq|\vec{a}|+r\leq L
and
0\leq r\leq 1
, we recall the chain rule and definition of the prime weighted norm from Proposition
4.2
(choosing the inverse timescale
\zeta=\Xi e_{u}^{1/2}
) to obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\overline{D}_{t}^{r}

\displaystyle[G(R_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n}),\nabla\check{\xi}_{k})-G(\tilde{R}_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n}),\nabla\xi_{k})]\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+r+1-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{r}\left(\overline{H}^{\prime}[G(R_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n}),\nabla\check{\xi}_{k})]+\widetilde{H}^{\prime}[G(\tilde{R}_{(n)}/(M_{e}D_{R,n}),\nabla\xi_{k})]\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\widehat{N}^{(|\vec{a}|+r+1-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{r}.

\displaystyle\leq N^{(|\vec{a}|+r+1-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|+3r/2}D_{u}^{r/2}

In the first inequality we use the Interchange Lemma. In the second inequality we used that the prime weighted norms of the arguments of
G
are
\lesssim 1
by the estimates we have proven for the stress and phase gradients (which indeed is the rationale for the definition of the prime weighted norm).

By definition of the final weighted norm, our goal is to bound this quantity by

(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}(N\Xi)^{\frac{3r}{2}}D_{R}^{r/2}[N^{-1/2}(D_{u}/D_{R})^{-1/2}]

In the cases
r=0
,
|\vec{a}|=1
as well as
r=1
,
|\vec{a}|=0
, the desired bound reduces to
N\geq D_{u}/D_{R}
, which follows from (
7
). The cases where
|\vec{a}|\geq 1
follow by inducting on
|\vec{a}|
; hence we obtain (
234
).
∎

We now estimate
R_{SI}
with the product rule and Proposition (
7.2
) to obtain

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[R_{SI}]

\displaystyle\lesssim H^{*}[g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)]H^{*}[e_{n}(t)]H^{*}[\chi_{k}^{2}(t)]\cdot

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[\gamma_{f}^{2}(p_{I})B^{j\ell}(\nabla\xi_{I})-\gamma_{f}^{2}(\check{p}_{I})B^{j\ell}(\nabla\check{\xi}_{I})]

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\cdot D_{R}\cdot 1\cdot(D_{u}/D_{R})^{-1/2}N^{-1/2}.

Here
H^{*}[\chi_{k}^{2}]\lesssim 1
because differentiating
\chi_{k}
costs
\tau^{-1}\leq\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}
. We also used Proposition
7.2

We now consider the
\delta B_{I}
term, which has already been considered and well-estimated in
[
31
, Proposition 4.5]
.
In order to apply that Proposition, we make the following changes in notation: we replace
\lambda=B_{\lambda}N\Xi
by our
\lambda=N\Xi
, we replace the coarse scale velocity field by
\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}
, its material derivative by
\tilde{D}_{t}
, the number of “unborrowed” derivatives by
{\underline{L}}
instead of
L
, the stress level for each
I=(f,k,n)
by
D_{R,n}
, and the cost of the (first) advective derivative by
\tau^{-1}
. The hypotheses of
[
31
, Proposition 4.5]
are then supplied by our (
176
) and Propositions
4.4
and
4.5
. Applying that proposition directly gives an estimate of the form

\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}^{r}\delta B_{I}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim_{\vec{a}}N^{\frac{3}{2{\underline{L}}}(|\vec{a}|+2-{\underline{L}})_{+}}\Xi^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-r}N^{-1}D_{R,n}

for
r=0,1
, which is sharper than we need. It suffices to observe that, for
0\leq r\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}^{r}\delta B_{I}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\vec{a}}(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-r}N^{-1}D_{R,n}\leq(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\tau^{-r}N^{-1}D_{R}.

(235)

Differentiating the external profile
g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)
costs
\mu\leq\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}
, so the final weighted norm gives

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{*}[g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)\delta B_{I}]\lesssim\tilde{H}^{*}[g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)]\tilde{H}^{*}[\delta B_{I}]

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\cdot N^{-1}D_{R}.

(236)

Thus our final bound on the stress error is

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[R_{S}]

\displaystyle\lesssim(D_{u}/D_{R})^{-1/2}N^{-1/2}D_{R}+N^{-1}D_{R}

\displaystyle\lesssim(D_{u}/D_{R})^{-1/2}N^{-1/2}D_{R},

(237)

since
N\geq D_{u}/D_{R}
.

8
Nonstationary phase

The transport term and the high-frequency interference terms are both high frequency and our treatment involves nonstationary phase, which is a by now a standard tool in convex integration arguments. Interestingly, this application of nonstationary phase and the power loss it gives rise to can be avoided (see Section
8.2
).

We first introduce a weighted norm.

Definition 8.1
.

The nonstationary phase weighted norm of
F
is

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{M}^{\prime\prime}[F]

\displaystyle=\max_{r\leq 1}\max_{0\leq|\vec{a}|+r\leq M}\frac{\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}^{r}F\|_{C^{0}}}{(N^{1/2}\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}}

(238)

Lemma 8.1
.

The
\tilde{H}_{M}^{\prime\prime}
norm satisfies the usual product rule and chain rules. Also, one has

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{M-1}^{\prime\prime}[\lambda^{-1}\nabla_{i}F]

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1/2}\tilde{H}_{M}[F]

(239)

Proof.

We omit the proof of the product and chain rules, since they are almost identical to the proof of Propositions
4.1
and
5.1
. As for (
239
), the bound on spatial derivatives is immediate from the definition, so we need only bound

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}\nabla_{i}F

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\vec{a}}\nabla_{i}\tilde{D}_{t}F+\overset{\sim}{\sum}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}\nabla_{i}\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}^{b}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{2}}\nabla_{b}F

where the sum ranges over
|\vec{a}_{1}|+|\vec{a}_{2}|=|\vec{a}|\leq M-1
. We bound this sum by

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\tilde{D}_{t}\nabla_{i}F\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|+1}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{M}[F]+\overset{\sim}{\sum}(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\Xi)^{|\vec{a}_{1}|+|\vec{a}_{2}|+1}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{M}[F]

\displaystyle\lesssim(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|+1}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{M}[F]

Dividing by
\lambda\sim N\Xi
yields the result.
∎

Proposition 8.1

(Nonstationary phase)
.

For any
D>0
there is a constant
C_{D}
so that the following holds. Whenever
G=e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}g
has integral
0
there is a traceless symmetric tensor field
Q^{j\ell}
that satisfies
\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}Q^{j\ell}=G
and the bound

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{*}[Q]

\displaystyle\leq C_{D}((N\Xi)^{-2}+N^{-D/2})\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L}[g]

(240)

Proof.

Consider the function
q^{j\ell}(p)=A|p|^{-4}p^{j}p^{\ell}+B|p|^{-2}\delta^{j\ell}
. Then if
A
and
B
solve the equations
A+B=1
and
A+dB=0
,
d=2
, we have that
q^{j\ell}(p)
is trace-free and satisfies
p_{j}p_{\ell}q^{j\ell}(p)=1
.

We write
Q^{j\ell}=Q_{(D)}^{j\ell}+\tilde{Q}_{(D)}^{j\ell}
where

\displaystyle Q_{(D)}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-2}\sum_{k=0}^{D}e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}q_{(k)}^{j\ell}

(241)

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{(D)}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{j\ell}[G-\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}Q_{(D)}^{j\ell}]

(242)

We define the
q_{(k)}^{j\ell}
recursively by

\displaystyle g_{(0)}

\displaystyle=g,\quad\qquad q_{(k)}^{j\ell}=q^{j\ell}(\nabla\xi_{I})g_{(k)}

(243)

\displaystyle g_{(k+1)}

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{-1}[\nabla_{j}\xi_{I}\nabla_{\ell}q_{(k)}^{j\ell}+\nabla_{\ell}\xi_{I}\nabla_{j}q_{(k)}^{j\ell}]]-\lambda^{-2}\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}q_{(k)}^{j\ell}

(244)

These inductive rules are defined so that

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}Q_{(D)}-e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}g

\displaystyle=e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}g_{(D+1)}^{j\ell}

(245)

We claim the following estimates inductively on
k
.

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2k}[g_{(k)}]

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-k/2}\tilde{H}_{2D+L}^{\prime\prime}[g]

(246)

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2k}[q_{(k)}]

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-k/2}\tilde{H}_{2D+L}^{\prime\prime}[g]

(247)

Indeed (
246
) holds for
k=0
trivially. Then (
247
) holds for
k
by

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2k}[q_{(k)}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2k}[q^{j\ell}(\nabla\xi_{I})]\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2k}[g_{(k)}]

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2k}[\nabla\xi_{I}])^{2D}\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L}[g]

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\cdot\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L}[g]

where we applied the product rule and chain rule for the weighted norm and the inductive hypothesis for
g_{(k)}
. Now we estimate (
244
) by the product rule, (
247
) for
k
, and (
239
)

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2(k+1)}[g_{(k+1)}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2(k+1)}[\nabla\xi_{I}]\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2(k+1)}[\lambda^{-1}\nabla q_{(k)}]

\displaystyle+\lambda^{-2}\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2(k+1)}[\nabla\nabla q_{(k)}]

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\cdot\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2k-1}[\lambda^{-1}\nabla q_{(k)}]+\lambda^{-1}\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2k-1}[\nabla q_{(k)}]

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1/2}\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2k}[\lambda^{-1}\nabla q_{(k)}]\lesssim N^{-(k+1)/2}\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L}[g],

which concludes the induction.

We can now estimate the
Q_{(D)}
defined in (
241
) by first observing

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}]

\displaystyle\lesssim 1

(248)

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[F]

\displaystyle\lesssim\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{L}[F]

(249)

We postpone the proof of (
248
). The second of these bounds is directly from the definition. We now estimate

\displaystyle\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[Q_{(D)}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-2}\sum_{k=0}^{D}\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}]\accentset{\ast}{H}^{*}[q_{(k)}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:sizeOfPhase}}}{{\lesssim}}\lambda^{-2}\sum_{k=0}^{D}\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{L}[q_{(k)}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-2}\sum_{k=0}^{D}\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L-2k}[q_{(k)}]\lesssim\lambda^{-2}\tilde{H}_{2D+L}^{\prime\prime}[g].

This bound suffices to prove (
240
) for the parametrix.

To bound the error, we need a trivial bound for the operator
\mathcal{R}^{j\ell}
. Specifically

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{*}[\mathcal{R}^{j\ell}[F]]

\displaystyle\lesssim\tilde{H}^{*}[F],

(250)

and we also will use

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{*}[e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}]

\displaystyle\lesssim 1.

(251)

Taking these two estimates as given, we now have

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{*}[\tilde{Q}_{(D)}]=\tilde{H}^{*}[\mathcal{R}^{j\ell}[e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}g_{(D+1)}]]

\displaystyle\lesssim\tilde{H}^{*}[e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}g_{(D+1)}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\tilde{H}^{*}[e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}]\tilde{H}^{*}[g_{(D+1)}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:inductgk}}}{{\lesssim}}1\cdot N^{-D/2}\tilde{H}_{2D+L}^{\prime\prime}[g],

which completes the proof subject to (
250
) and (
251
).
∎

Proof of (
250
) and (
251
).

To prove (
251
), we observe that
\tilde{D}_{t}e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}=0
, so it suffices to bound spatial derivatives. By the chain rule and product rule we obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{L}\underset{\vec{a}_{j}}{\overset{\sim}{\sum}}\|e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}\|_{C^{0}}\lambda^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{j}}\xi_{I}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{L}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\lambda^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{\Xi}^{|\vec{a}_{j}|-1}

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{L}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\lambda^{|\vec{a}_{j}|}\lesssim\lambda^{|\vec{a}|},

where we have used that derivatives of
\xi
cost at most
\widehat{\Xi}
, which is smaller than
\lambda
.

To prove (
250
), we first note that
\mathcal{R}^{j\ell}
is bounded on
C^{0}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. For example,

\|\mathcal{R}^{j\ell}\|\leq\sum_{q}\|P_{q}\mathcal{R}^{j\ell}\|\lesssim\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}2^{-2q}

Then
H^{*}[\mathcal{R}^{j\ell}F]\lesssim H^{*}[F]
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{R}
commutes with
\nabla_{\vec{a}}
and
\partial_{t}
. By comparability of weighted norms, this estimate suffices.
∎

8.1
High frequency error terms

We now apply the nonstationary phase estimate to the high frequency error terms.

We start with
R_{H}
. There is an important cancellation in this term that was first observed in
[
34
]
. Namely, since
u_{I}^{a}\nabla_{a}\xi_{I}=\theta_{I}m^{a}(\nabla\xi_{I})\nabla_{a}\xi_{I}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{I}T^{a}\Theta_{I}\nabla_{a}\Theta_{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{I}\lambda g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)e^{2i\lambda\xi_{I}}\delta u_{I}^{a}(i\nabla_{a}\xi_{I})(\theta_{I}+\delta\theta_{I})

\displaystyle+\sum_{I}g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)e^{2i\lambda\xi_{I}}(u_{I}^{a}+\delta u_{I}^{a})(\nabla_{a}\theta_{I}+\nabla_{a}\delta\theta_{I})

For the next computation, let
\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}
be a shorthand for
\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}_{2D+L}
. By nonstationary phase, for any
D\geq 0
there exists a traceless second-order anti-divergence that obeys the estimate

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{*}[R_{H}^{j\ell}]

\displaystyle\lesssim(\lambda^{-2}+N^{-D/2})(A+B)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\lambda\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)]\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[\delta u_{I}]\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[\nabla\xi_{I}](\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[\theta_{I}]+\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[\delta\theta_{I}])

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[g_{[I]}^{2}(\mu t)]\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[u_{I}+\delta u_{I}]\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[\nabla\theta_{I}+\nabla\delta\theta_{I}]

For all these terms inside the weighted norms, we claim that the bounds for the
H^{\prime\prime}
norm of each term are the same as the bound we have stated for the
C^{0}
norm of each term. Indeed, for each of these terms, a spatial derivative costs at most
\widehat{\Xi}=N^{1/L}\Xi
, which is smaller than
N^{1/2}\Xi
, while an advective derivative costs at most
\mu=\Xi^{3/2}N^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}
, which is smaller than
\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}=(N\Xi)^{3/2}D_{R}^{1/2}
. Combining (
206
), Proposition
4.4
, Proposition
4.5
, and the following estimate

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[u_{I}^{\ell}]

\displaystyle=\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[m^{\ell}(\nabla\xi_{I})]\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[\theta_{I}]\lesssim\tilde{H}^{\prime\prime}[\theta_{I}]\lesssim\lambda^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}

yields (recall
\lambda=N\Xi
)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)\cdot 1\cdot\frac{\lambda^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}}{N}1(\lambda^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2})

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\cdot\lambda^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}(\Xi\lambda^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2})

Hence we conclude,

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{*}[R_{H}^{j\ell}]

\displaystyle\lesssim(\lambda^{-2}+N^{-D/2})(\lambda\Xi D_{R})

(252)

Recall that
N\geq N^{4\eta}\Xi^{4\eta}\sim\lambda^{4\eta}
. Choosing
D
large depending on
\eta
, we have
\tilde{H}^{*}[R_{H}^{j\ell}]\lesssim\frac{D_{R}}{N}
.

The other high frequency term is the transport term. Since the advective derivative annihilates the phase function, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}R_{T}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{I}\mu g_{[I]}^{\prime}(\mu t)e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}(\theta_{I}+\delta\theta_{I})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{I}g_{[I]}(\mu t)e^{i\lambda\xi_{I}}\left[\tilde{D}_{t}\theta_{I}+\tilde{D}_{t}\delta\theta_{I}+(u_{I}^{a}+\delta u_{I}^{a})\nabla_{a}\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon}\right].

Nonstationary phase with the same choice of
D
as before yields a solution of weighted norm

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{*}[R_{T}]

\displaystyle\lesssim(\lambda^{-2}+N^{-D/2})\Big\{\mu H^{\prime\prime}[g_{[I]}^{\prime}(\mu t)]H^{\prime\prime}[\theta_{I}+\delta\theta_{I}]

\displaystyle\qquad+H^{\prime\prime}[g_{[I]}(\mu t)]\big(H^{\prime\prime}[\tilde{D}_{t}\theta_{I}]+H^{\prime\prime}[\tilde{D}_{t}\delta\theta_{I}]+H^{\prime\prime}[u_{I}+\delta u_{I}]H^{\prime\prime}[\nabla\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon}]\big)\Big\}.

For these terms it is again true that the
H^{\prime\prime}
weighted norm is the same size as the bound on the
C^{0}
norm modulo constants, since spatial derivatives cost at most
N^{1/L}\Xi<N^{1/2}\Xi
, while the additional material derivative costs at most

\epsilon_{t}^{-1}=(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})(D_{u}/D_{R})^{1/2}N^{1/2}\leq\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}=(N\Xi)^{3/2}D_{R}^{1/2}.

The first material derivative of
\theta_{I}
retains the cutoff-driven cost
\tau^{-1}
. Combining (
206
)-(
207
), Proposition
4.4
, and Proposition
4.5
, we therefore obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{*}[R_{T}]

\displaystyle\lesssim(\lambda^{-2}+N^{-D/2})\Big[\mu\lambda^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}+\tau^{-1}\lambda^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}+\lambda^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}\,\Xi e_{u}^{1/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim(\lambda^{-2}+N^{-D/2})\mu\lambda^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}\lesssim N^{-1}D_{R}.

Both the estimate for
R_{H}
and the estimate for
R_{T}
are satisfactory for the Main Lemma, since we have
N^{-1}D_{R}\leq(D_{u}/D_{R})^{-1/2}N^{-1/2}D_{R}
.

8.2
How to avoid nonstationary phase

We include this section to note that one can avoid nonstationary phase in the proof in a way such that the only source of double exponential frequency growth occurs during the Newton step. To do so, let
\tilde{u}^{\ell}=u_{\epsilon}^{\ell}+T^{\ell}w
, where
w
is the Newton correction. Instead of transporting the phase functions by the flow of
\tilde{u}^{\ell}
as we have done, first apply a frequency truncation
P_{\leq q_{\epsilon}}
to
T^{\ell}w
and transport the phase functions by the resulting frequency localized vector field.

With such a frequency localization, both the high frequency interference terms and the transport term now live at frequency
\sim\lambda
, and one can simply apply an operator
{\cal R}^{j\ell}
to both those terms to find a suitable anti-divergence that gains a smallness of
\lambda^{-2}
. This technique avoids nonstationary phase (which was also avoided in
[
34
,
7
,
31
]
), and also avoids the power loss in frequency incurred during the nonstationary phase, which is important for deriving an endpoint type result
[
36
]
. However, it comes with two complications.

1.

The estimates of the convex integration step are a bit different in terms of powers of
N
although the final bounds for
\accentset{\ast}{R}
are the same.

2.

One has to handle an error term of the form

\displaystyle(T^{\ell}w-P_{\leq q_{\epsilon}}T^{\ell}w)\Theta+T^{\ell}\Theta(w-P_{\leq q_{\epsilon}}w)

The latter term can be treated similarly to the mollification term addressed below.

8.3
The mollification error

The term
R_{M}
is also a new error term compared to
[
7
,
31
]
. In those works there was no need to regularize
\theta
since it could be enforced that
\theta
had compact frequency support. In other words, we had
\theta=\theta_{\epsilon}
. Here
\theta
does not have compact frequency support, so we have to bound this term, which resembles the term (
40
). Again we use our simplified version of the observation in
[
32
]
showing how to write the nonlinearity in a divergence form.

We are estimating a solution to

\displaystyle\nabla_{\ell}R_{M}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=[T^{j}\theta-T^{j}\theta_{\epsilon}]\Theta+[T^{j}\Theta](\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})

(253)

We know
\mathrm{supp}\,\hat{\Theta}\subset\{\xi~:~\lambda/10^{2}\leq|\xi|\leq 10^{2}\lambda\}
.
We decompose
\nabla_{j}R_{M}^{j\ell}
into the sum of three kinds of terms (HH, HL and LH) (or really five kinds of terms, but we can group them into three kinds).

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla_{j}R^{j\ell}_{M}&=\sum_{q}P_{q}T^{\ell}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})P_{q+1}\Theta+T^{\ell}P_{q+1}\Theta P_{q}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})+\text{ Similar}\\
&+\sum_{q}P_{\leq q-1}T^{\ell}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})P_{q+1}\Theta\\
&+\sum_{q}P_{\leq q-1}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})T^{\ell}P_{q+1}\Theta\\
&+\sum_{q}P_{q+1}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})T^{\ell}P_{\leq q-1}\Theta\\
&+\sum_{q}P_{q+1}T^{\ell}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})P_{\leq q-1}\Theta\end{split}

(254)

(Recall that
T^{\ell}
and
P_{k}
commute for any
k
, and same for
P_{\leq k-1}
)

Define
q^{\lambda}\in\mathbb{N}
by
q^{\lambda}:\approx\log_{2}(\lambda)
. We have that the Fourier support of
\hat{\Theta}
is essentially in a single dyadic shell (or a bounded number of shells). By consideration of frequency support, the five sums can be simplified as sums over, respectively,

q\sim q^{\lambda},q\sim q^{\lambda},q\sim q^{\lambda},q\geq q^{\lambda},q\geq q^{\lambda}.

To each of these we associate an antidivergence, and we just have to bound that antidivergence. We will only do this for three cases (one being the High-High), which are representative of all five.

Looking at the very first term in (
254
), we use the divergence form principle of Section
A.2
to define

\displaystyle R_{MHq}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=K_{q}\ast[\theta-\theta_{\epsilon},\Theta]

(255)

\displaystyle=K_{q}\ast[P_{\approx q^{\lambda}}\theta,\Theta]

(256)

\displaystyle=\int(\theta(x-h_{1})-\theta_{\epsilon}(x-h_{1}))\Theta(x-h_{2})K_{q}^{j\ell}(h_{1},h_{2})dh_{1}dh_{2}

(257)

For all
0\leq r+|\vec{a}|\leq L
we bound

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\partial_{t}^{r}R_{MHq}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{q\sim q^{\lambda}}\|K_{q}\|_{L^{1}}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}\partial_{t}^{r_{1}}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})\|_{0}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}\partial_{t}^{r_{1}}\Theta\|_{0}

(258)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{q\sim q^{\lambda}}2^{-q}[(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}_{1}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{1}}\frac{e_{u}^{1/2}}{N}][(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}_{2}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{2}}H^{*}[\Theta]]

(259)

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{-1}(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}\frac{e_{u}^{1/2}}{N}(N\Xi)^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}.

(260)

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}\frac{D_{R}}{N},

(261)

where in the last line we used
N\geq D_{u}/D_{R}
, while in the second line we used Lemma
3.7
and the bound on
\Theta
obtained in the proof of Proposition
5.2
.

Our next representative term is

\displaystyle R_{MHL}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\sim q_{\lambda}}\mathcal{R}_{a}^{j\ell}[P_{q+1}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})P_{\leq q-1}T^{a}\Theta]

(262)

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\sim q_{\lambda}}\mathcal{R}_{a}^{j\ell}P_{\approx q}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})P_{\approx\lambda}T^{a}\Theta

(263)

where the representation in the second line is due to the Fourier support of
\Theta
being in
|\xi|\sim\lambda
. We bound this term by

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\partial_{t}^{r}\eqref{eq:MollHL}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{q\sim q^{\lambda}}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\|\mathcal{R}_{a}^{j\ell}P_{\approx q}\|[(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}_{1}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{1}}\frac{e_{u}^{1/2}}{N}][(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}_{2}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{2}}H^{*}[\Theta]]

(264)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{q\geq q_{\lambda}}2^{-q}(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}\frac{e_{u}^{1/2}}{N}(N\Xi)^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}

(265)

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}\frac{D_{R}}{N}.

(266)

Here in the second line we used the trivial observation that
H[T^{\ell}P_{q}\Theta]\lesssim H[\Theta]
by the fact that
T^{\ell}P_{q}
is bounded on
C^{0}
and commutes with spatial derivatives and
\partial_{t}
. In the last line we again used
N\geq D_{u}/D_{R}
.

The last of the three representative terms is

\displaystyle\sum_{q\sim a^{\lambda}}P_{\leq q-1}T^{\ell}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})P_{q+1}\Theta.

(267)

We write

\displaystyle R_{MLH}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\sim q^{\lambda}}{\cal R}_{a}^{j\ell}P_{\approx q}[P_{\leq q-1}T^{a}(\theta-\theta_{\epsilon})P_{q+1}\Theta]

(268)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\partial_{t}^{r}\eqref{eq:hardMollTerm}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{q\sim q^{\lambda}}\lambda^{-1}\overset{\sim}{\sum}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}\partial_{t}^{r_{1}}(T\theta-T\theta_{\epsilon})\|_{C^{0}}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{2}}\partial_{t}^{r_{2}}\Theta\|_{C^{0}}

(269)

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\overset{\sim}{\sum}[(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}_{1}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{1}}\frac{e_{u}^{1/2}}{N}][(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}_{2}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r_{2}}H[\Theta]]

(270)

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{-1}(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}\frac{e_{u}^{1/2}}{N}(N\Xi)^{1/2}D_{R}^{1/2}

(271)

\displaystyle\lesssim(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-r}\frac{D_{R}}{N}

(272)

Here again we used Lemma
3.7
, the bound on
\Theta
obtained in the proof of Proposition
5.2
, and
N\geq D_{u}/D_{R}
.

Combining these estimates we have

\displaystyle H^{*}[R_{M}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{D_{R}}{N}.

(273)

9
The Main Lemma implies the Main Theorem

We start with the following auxiliary theorem, which is enough to prove regularity of solutions but in itself is not enough to prove nontriviality. Nontriviality will be a corollary of the h-principle.

For any
\delta>0
given, we choose
L\geq 7
and
\eta>0
depending on
\delta
so that the parameter

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\frac{6}{L}+4\eta\leq\delta^{3}.

(274)

Let
\widehat{C}
be the constant in the Main Lemma associated to this choice of
\eta,L
.

Theorem 4
.

Let
\delta>0
be given. There is a constant
C_{\delta}
depending on
\delta
and an integer
L
such that the following holds. Let
(\theta_{0},R_{0})
be an SQG-Reynolds flow with frequency energy levels of order
L
bounded by
(\Xi_{0},D_{u,0},D_{R,0})
and with
\mathrm{supp}_{t}\,R_{0}
compactly contained in a nonempty set
J_{0}
. Then there exists a solution
\theta
to SQG of class
|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta\in C^{1/2-2\delta}

such that
\mathrm{supp}_{t}\,\theta-\theta_{0}
is contained in a
C_{\delta}\widehat{C}(\Xi_{0}e_{u,0}^{1/2})^{-1}
neighborhood of
J_{0}
and such that

\displaystyle\|~|\nabla|^{-1/2}(\theta-\theta_{0})\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\delta}D_{R,0}^{1/2}

(275)

Proof.

We define a sequence of SQG-Reynolds flows
(\theta_{n},R_{n})
by iteration of the Main Lemma. We set
(\Xi,D_{u},D_{R})_{(0)}=(\Xi_{0},D_{u,0},D_{R,0})
and evolve according to the parameter rules

\displaystyle\Xi_{(k+1)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}N_{(k)}\Xi_{(k)}

\displaystyle D_{u,(k+1)}

\displaystyle=D_{R,(k)}

\displaystyle D_{R,(k+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{D_{R,(k)}^{1+\delta}}{Z}.

where
Z
is to be chosen depending on
\widehat{C}
and on the initial frequency energy levels.

Remark 4
.

We note that, following
[
35
]
, the above parameter evolution rules are essentially the simplest formulas that guarantee a slight double exponential growth of frequencies, while allowing an arbitrarily large stress at the initial stage of the iteration, which is essential to Theorem
4
.

The above parameter evolution rules will imply a double exponential decay of
D_{R,(k)}
, but for the moment we impose that
Z\geq\max\{2D_{R,(0)}^{\delta},D_{R,(0)}^{1+\delta}\}
in order to ensure that
D_{R,(1)}\leq 1
and that

\displaystyle D_{R,(k+1)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}D_{R,(k)}

(276)

for all
k
.

Our choice of
N_{(k)}
is dictated by the estimate in the Main Lemma:

\displaystyle D_{R,(k+1)}

\displaystyle={\widehat{C}}\left(\frac{D_{u}}{D_{R}}\right)_{(k)}^{-1/2}N^{-1/2}_{(k)}D_{R,(k)}

(277)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow N_{(k)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}^{2}Z^{2}(D_{u}/D_{R})_{(k)}^{-1}D_{R(k)}^{-2\delta}.

(278)

The most delicate task in this framework is to check that
N_{(k)}
is admissible, since this condition barely holds (see Remark
1
); namely, we need

\displaystyle N_{(k)}\geq\widehat{C}N_{(k)}^{\frac{6}{L}+4\eta}\Xi_{(k)}^{4\eta}(D_{u}/D_{R})_{(k)}.

(279)

With
\varepsilon=\frac{6}{L}+4\eta
defined as above and substituting (
278
), it is enough to check that

\displaystyle Z^{-2+2\varepsilon}\Xi_{(k)}^{\varepsilon}(D_{u}/D_{R})_{(k)}^{2-\varepsilon}D_{R,(k)}^{2\delta(1-\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\leq 1

(280)

Since the power of
Z
is negative, it is clear that
Z
can be chosen large enough so that this inequality holds at
k=0
. Now suppose
k\geq 1
. In this case one has

\displaystyle(D_{u}/D_{R})_{(k)}=(D_{R,(k-1)}/D_{R,(k)})

\displaystyle=Z^{\frac{1}{1+\delta}}D_{R,(k)}^{-\delta/(1+\delta)}

(281)

Taking logs of (
280
), we need to check:

\displaystyle(-2+2\varepsilon+\frac{2-\varepsilon}{1+\delta})\log Z+\varepsilon\log\Xi_{(k)}+(2\delta(1-\varepsilon)-\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}(2-\varepsilon))\log D_{R,(k)}

\displaystyle\leq 0

(282)

We prove this inequality by induction, as we have already considered the case
k=0
. Since
Z
is a constant, we need only check that

\displaystyle\varepsilon\delta_{(k)}\log\Xi_{(k)}+\delta\left(2(1-\varepsilon)-\frac{1}{1+\delta}(2-\varepsilon)\right)\delta_{(k)}\log D_{R,(k)}

(283)

is negative, where
\delta_{(k)}f=f_{(k+1)}-f_{(k)}
denotes the discrete difference operator. Note that since
\delta_{(k)}\log\Xi_{(k)}=\log(\Xi_{(k+1)}/\Xi_{(k)})=\log(\widehat{C}N_{(k)})
and
(D_{u}/D_{R})_{(k)}^{-1}\leq 1\leq Z
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{(k)}\log\Xi_{(k)}=\log(\widehat{C}^{3}Z^{2}(D_{u}/D_{R})_{(k)}^{-1}D_{R(k)}^{-2\delta})\leq\log(\widehat{C}^{3}Z^{3}D_{R,(k)}^{-2\delta})

(284)

We now use that
\varepsilon=O(\delta^{3})
,
D_{R,(k)}\leq 1
for all
k\geq 1
, and we recall
D_{R,(k+1)}=(D_{R,(k)}^{\delta}/Z)D_{R,(k)}
to obtain

(
283
)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\delta_{(k)}\log\Xi_{(k)}+(2\delta^{2}+O(\delta^{3})+O(\varepsilon))\delta_{(k)}\log D_{R,(k)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:XiChangeIneq}}}{{\leq}}3\varepsilon(\log\widehat{C}+\log Z)-2\delta\varepsilon\log D_{R,(k)}+(2\delta^{2}+O(\delta^{3}))(\delta\log D_{R,(k)}-\log Z)

\displaystyle\leq O(\delta^{3})\log\widehat{C}+(-2\delta^{2}+O(\delta^{3}))\log Z\qquad\qquad\mbox{ since }\log D_{R,(k)}\leq 0.

We now choose
Z
depending on
\delta
and
\widehat{C}
so that the right hand side is negative as desired. Thus our choice of
N_{(k)}
is admissible for all
k
.

Applying (
276
), our solution
\theta
obeys

\displaystyle\||\nabla|^{-1/2}(\theta-\theta_{0})\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\||\nabla|^{-1/2}W_{(k)}\|_{C^{0}}

(285)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}D_{R,(k)}^{1/2}\leq C_{\delta}D_{R,(0)}^{1/2}.

(286)

Note that the convergence of this series combined with boundedness of the nonlinearity in
L_{t}^{2}\dot{H}^{-1/2}
also shows that
\theta
is a weak solution to SQG. A similar geometric series bounds the size of the increase in time support of
\theta_{(k)}-\theta_{0}
by

\displaystyle\sum_{k}(\Xi_{(k)}e_{u,(k)}^{1/2})^{-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\Xi_{0}e_{u,0}^{1/2})^{-1}

(287)

hence the time support of
\theta-\theta_{0}
is bounded as claimed.

To check the regularity of the solution, we follow the method of
[
35
]
. It will be convenient to phrase the parameter evolution rules in terms of logs

\displaystyle\psi_{(k)}

\displaystyle\equiv[\log D_{R(k)},\log(D_{u}/D_{R})_{(k)},\log\Xi_{(k)}]^{t}

(288)

\displaystyle\psi_{(k+1)}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}-\log Z\\
\log Z\\
\log(\widehat{C}^{3}Z^{2})\end{bmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}1+\delta&0&0\\
-\delta&0&0\\
-2\delta&-1&1\end{pmatrix}\psi_{(k)}

(289)

We call the
3\times 3

parameter evolution matrix
appearing here
T_{\delta}
.

We seek a vector in the null space of
T_{\delta}-(1+\delta)
with a negative first coordinate, since the largest component of
\psi_{(k)}
will be in that direction. An example is given by

\displaystyle\psi_{+}=\begin{bmatrix}-(1+\delta)\\
\delta\\
1+2\delta\end{bmatrix}

(290)

In terms of eigenvectors
(\psi_{+},\psi_{0},\psi_{1})
for the
(1+\delta,0,1)
eigenspaces respectively, we can decompose

\displaystyle\psi_{(k)}

\displaystyle=c_{+,(k)}\psi_{+}+c_{0,(k)}\psi_{0}+c_{1,(k)}\psi_{1}

(291)

where the coefficients evolve by diagonalizing (
289
)

\displaystyle c_{+(k+1)}

\displaystyle=(1+\delta)c_{+(k)}+\ell_{+}

\displaystyle c_{1(k+1)}

\displaystyle=1c_{1,(k)}+\ell_{1}

\displaystyle c_{0(k+1)}

\displaystyle=0c_{0(k)}+\ell_{0}

for some constants
\ell_{+},\ell_{1}
, and
\ell_{0}
.

As we are essentially iteratively applying a fixed matrix, one expects the contribution from the
(1+\delta)
eigenspace to be the largest, and this is indeed the case. It is easy to check by induction that
c_{1(k)}
and
c_{0(k)}
are both bounded by
O(k)
. Meanwhile
c_{+(k)}
yields the main term. In particular,

\displaystyle c_{+(k)}\geq c(1+\delta)^{k}

(292)

for some
c>0
. To see this it suffices to note that
\ell_{+}>0
, which can be checked by applying the
(1+\delta)
row eigenvector
[1,0,0]
(which annihilates
\psi_{0}
and
\psi_{1}
) to (
289
) and substituting (
291
).

We now compute the regularity of our solution. Using the interpolation inequality
\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}}\lesssim\|f\|_{C^{0}}^{1-\alpha}\|\nabla f\|_{C^{0}}^{\alpha}
, the estimate (
10
) on
W
, the formula (
278
) for
N_{(k)}
and
\alpha<1
, one has

\displaystyle\log\||\nabla|^{-1/2}W\|_{C^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq\log\widehat{C}+\alpha\log(N_{(k)}\Xi_{(k)})+\frac{1}{2}\log D_{R,(k)}

(293)

\displaystyle\leq\log(\widehat{C}^{4}Z^{2})+\left[\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha\delta,-\alpha,\alpha\right]\psi_{(k)},

(294)

where the last line refers to the linear pairing of the row vector with the column vector
\psi_{(k)}
. From (
291
) and (
292
), we see that the right hand side goes to
-\infty
exponentially fast whenever the same row vector applied to
\psi_{+}
in (
290
) gives a negative value. In conclusion,
|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta\in L_{t}^{\infty}C^{\alpha}
whenever

\displaystyle\alpha<\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1+\delta}{1+3\delta+2\delta^{2}}\right).

Using linearization, one sees that
\alpha=1/2-2\delta
satisfies this inequality for
\delta
sufficiently small, hence Theorem
4
is proven.
∎

9.1
Nonuniqueness for the initial value problem

We now prove Theorem
2
following the argument in
[
35
]
. Let
c>0
be given. Let
\theta_{0}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be
C^{\infty}
with zero mean and choose
T>0
such that the smooth classical solution to SQG, denoted by
f
, is defined on an open interval that compactly contains
[-T,T]
. Taking
c
smaller if needed, assume
[-(T+2c),T+2c]
is also compactly contained in the existence interval for the smooth solution
f
.

Now consider the scalar function

\displaystyle\theta_{0}=\chi(t)f

(295)

for some cutoff function
\chi(t)
equal to
1
on
[-T-c/5,T+c/5]
and equal to
0
on
|t|\geq T+c/2
. We can realize
\theta_{0}
as the scalar function part of a smooth SQG Reynolds flow

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta_{0}+\nabla_{j}[T^{j}[\theta_{0}]\theta_{0}]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}R^{j\ell}_{0}.

(296)

Indeed, it suffices to take

\displaystyle R_{0}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle\equiv{\cal R}^{j\ell}[\partial_{t}\theta_{0}+\nabla_{a}[T^{a}[\theta_{0}]\theta_{0}]={\cal R}^{j\ell}[\partial_{t}\chi f+(\chi^{2}-\chi)\nabla_{a}[T^{a}ff]]

(297)

Note that
R_{0}^{j\ell}
solves (
296
) if the right hand side of (
297
) has mean zero for all
t
, which does hold by the conservation of the integral for SQG solutions, and the fact that a divergence always has integral
0
. Moreover, if we set
D_{R}=\|R_{0}\|_{C^{0}}
, we have that
(\theta_{0},R_{0})
has frequency energy levels to order
L
in
C^{0}
below
(\Xi,D_{R},D_{R})
for all
\Xi
sufficiently large.

The existence of our desired SQG flow, which differs from
\theta_{0}
only in an arbitrarily small neighborhood of the set
[-T-c/2,-T-c/5]\cup[T+c/5,T+c/2]
that contains the support of the right hand side of (
297
), now follows immediately from the approximation theorem, Theorem
4
, by taking
\Xi
sufficiently large so that
\widehat{C}C_{\delta}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}=\widehat{C}C_{\delta}(\Xi^{3/2}D_{R}^{1/2})^{-1}
is smaller than
c/7
.

9.2
h-Principle

Let
\delta>0
be given and let
L
and
C_{\delta}
be as in Theorem
4
.

Let
f:(0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth compactly supported function that conserves the integral. That is,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}f(t,x)dx=0,\qquad\mbox{ for all }t.

We approximate
f
by the sequence
f_{n}=P_{\leq n}f
, which satisfy

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\partial_{t}^{r}f_{n}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}\partial_{t}^{r}f\|_{C^{0}},\quad\mbox{ for }0\leq|\vec{a}|,r

(298)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\||\nabla|^{-1/2}(f_{n}-f)\|_{C^{0}}=0.

(299)

Using the order
-2
operator
{\cal R}^{j\ell}
, define

\displaystyle R_{n}^{j\ell}

\displaystyle={\cal R}^{j\ell}[\partial_{t}f_{n}+\nabla_{a}[f_{n}T^{a}f_{n}]]

(300)

so that
(f_{n},R_{n})
define an SQG-Reynolds flow with compact frequency support. (It is important at this point that the right hand side has mean zero at every time.) Choose
D_{R,-1}>0
large enough such that

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\|R_{n}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq D_{R,-1}.

By (
298
) we can choose a fixed
\Xi_{-1}\geq 2
and a sequence
\Xi_{-1,n}\geq\Xi_{-1}
tending to
+\infty
so that
(f_{n},R_{n})
is an SQG-Reynolds flow with frequency energy levels to order
L
bounded by
(\Xi_{-1,n},D_{R,-1},D_{R,-1})
that has compact frequency support in frequencies below
\Xi_{-1,n}
. Set

e_{R,-1,n}^{1/2}:=\Xi_{-1,n}^{1/2}D_{R,-1}^{1/2},\qquad e_{R,-1}^{1/2}:=\Xi_{-1}^{1/2}D_{R,-1}^{1/2}.

We use
[
31
, Lemma 3.1, with frequency–energy levels as in Definition 2.2]
, which we now state in the present notation. Let
\Xi\geq 2
and
\mathring{D}_{u}\geq\mathring{D}_{R}>0
and let
N\geq\mathring{D}_{u}/\mathring{D}_{R}
. Let
(f_{-1},R_{-1})
be an SQG Reynolds flow with frequency energy levels to order
L
below
(\Xi,\mathring{D}_{u},\mathring{D}_{R})
and such that
\mathrm{supp}\,\hat{f}_{-1}\subseteq\{|\xi|\leq\Xi\}
and such that
\mathrm{supp}_{t}\,(f_{-1},R_{-1})\subseteq J
for some nonempty set
J
. Then there is a second SQG Reynolds flow
(\accentset{\ast}{f},\accentset{\ast}{R})
,
\accentset{\ast}{f}=f_{-1}+W
with frequency energy levels below

(\Xi^{*},\mathring{D}_{u}^{*},\mathring{D}_{R}^{*})=\left(C_{b}N\Xi,\mathring{D}_{R},G\mathring{D}_{R}\right),\qquad G=\left(\frac{\mathring{D}_{u}}{\mathring{D}_{R}}\right)^{1/4}N^{-3/4},

to order
L
in
C^{0}
that satisfies the following bounds:

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}\,_{t}(\accentset{\ast}{f},\accentset{\ast}{R})

\displaystyle\subseteq\{t+t^{\prime}~:~t\in J,|t^{\prime}|\leq 3(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}\}

(301)

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{\vec{a}}|\nabla|^{-1/2}W\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}(N\Xi)^{|\vec{a}|}D_{R}^{1/2},\qquad|\vec{a}|\leq 1,

(302)

\displaystyle|\nabla|^{-1/2}W

\displaystyle=\nabla_{i}Y^{i},\qquad\|Y^{i}\|_{C^{0}}\leq C_{L}(N\Xi)^{-1}D_{R}^{1/2}.

(303)

where
e_{u}^{1/2}=\Xi^{1/2}\mathring{D}_{u}^{1/2}
. The estimates in this quoted lemma are not as strong as those of the present paper, but it is mainly this last estimate on the anti-divergence that we will require for our specific application to the h-principle.

Now let
N_{-1,n}
be a sequence tending to
+\infty
such that
N_{-1,n}\geq C_{L}
.
Apply the above lemma with
(\Xi_{-1,n},D_{R,-1},D_{R,-1})
, the flow
(f_{n},R_{n})
, and the parameter
N_{-1,n}
. For each
n
this gives a second SQG–Reynolds flow
(\theta_{0,n},R_{0,n})
, with
\theta_{0,n}=f_{n}+W_{-1,n}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}\,_{t}(\theta_{0,n},R_{0,n})

\displaystyle\subseteq\{t+t^{\prime}~:~t\in\mathrm{supp}_{t}\,(f_{n},R_{n}),|t^{\prime}|\leq 3(\Xi_{-1,n}e_{R,-1,n}^{1/2})^{-1}\}

(304)

\displaystyle\||\nabla|^{-1/2}W_{-1,n}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}D_{R,-1}^{1/2}

(305)

\displaystyle|\nabla|^{-1/2}W_{-1,n}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{i}Y_{n}^{i},\qquad\|Y_{n}^{i}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\Xi_{-1,n}^{-1}D_{R,-1}^{1/2}

(306)

and so that the frequency energy levels of
(\theta_{0,n},R_{0,n})
are bounded to order
L
by

\displaystyle(\Xi_{n,(0)},D_{u,(0)},D_{R,(0)})

\displaystyle=\left(C_{L}N_{-1,n}\Xi_{-1,n},D_{R,(-1)},\frac{D_{R,-1}}{N_{-1,n}^{3/4}}\right).

We write
e_{u,n,(0)}^{1/2}:=\Xi_{n,(0)}^{1/2}D_{u,(0)}^{1/2}=\Xi_{n,(0)}^{1/2}D_{R,-1}^{1/2}
.
Now apply our approximation theorem, Theorem
4
, to get an SQG solution
\theta_{n}
of class
|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta_{n}\in L_{t}^{\infty}C^{1/2-2\delta}
with

\displaystyle\||\nabla|^{-1/2}(\theta_{n}-\theta_{0,n})\|_{C^{0}}\leq C_{\delta}\frac{D_{R,-1}^{1/2}}{N_{-1,n}^{3/8}}

(307)

and with time support contained in

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}\,_{t}\theta_{n}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{t+t^{\prime}~:~t\in\mathrm{supp}\,_{t}(\theta_{0,n},R_{0,n}),\quad|t^{\prime}|\leq C_{\delta}(\Xi_{n,(0)}e_{u,n,(0)}^{1/2})^{-1}\}.

(308)

We now claim that
|\nabla|^{-1/2}(\theta_{n}-f)\to 0
in
L^{\infty}
weak-*. To see this claim, let
g\in L^{1}((0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
and let
\epsilon>0
be given. We will choose a small parameter
\eta
depending on
\epsilon
. Choose a
g_{\eta}\in C_{c}^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
within
\eta
of
g
in
L^{1}
, and write

\displaystyle\int g|\nabla|^{-1/2}(f-\theta_{n})dx

\displaystyle=I+II+III

(309)

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\int(g-g_{\eta})|\nabla|^{-1/2}(f-\theta_{n})dx

(310)

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=\int g_{\eta}|\nabla|^{-1/2}(f-\theta_{0,n})dx

(311)

\displaystyle III

\displaystyle=\int g_{\eta}|\nabla|^{-1/2}(\theta_{0,n}-\theta_{n})dx

(312)

We bound

\displaystyle|I|

\displaystyle\leq\eta(\||\nabla|^{-1/2}f\|_{C^{0}}+\sup_{n}\||\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta_{n}\|_{C^{0}})

Note that the
\sup
exists due to (
305
) and (
307
). Now fix the choice of
\eta
so that this term is bounded by
\epsilon/3
.

Since
\theta_{0,n}=f_{n}+W_{-1,n}
and
|\nabla|^{-1/2}W_{-1,n}=\nabla_{i}Y_{n}^{i}
, integration by parts gives

\displaystyle|II|

\displaystyle\leq\|g_{\eta}\|_{L^{1}}\,\big\||\nabla|^{-1/2}(f-f_{n})\big\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla g_{\eta}\|_{L^{1}}\,\|Y_{n}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\|g_{\eta}\|_{L^{1}}\,\big\||\nabla|^{-1/2}(f-f_{n})\big\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla g_{\eta}\|_{L^{1}}\frac{D_{R,-1}^{1/2}}{\Xi_{-1,n}}.

Both terms tend to zero by (
299
) and the choice
\Xi_{-1,n}\to\infty
.

Finally we have

\displaystyle|III|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int|g_{\eta}|\,dx\right)\||\nabla|^{-1/2}(\theta_{0,n}-\theta_{n})\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int|g_{\eta}|\,dx\right)C_{\delta}D_{R,-1}^{1/2}N_{-1,n}^{-3/8}

As long as we take
N_{-1,n}
to go to infinity, this term is also arbitrarily small. From this estimate we conclude that
|\nabla|^{-1/2}\theta_{n}\to|\nabla|^{-1/2}f
in
L^{\infty}
weak-*. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}_{t}\,\theta_{n}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{t+t^{\prime}~:~t\in\mathrm{supp}_{t}\,f,|t^{\prime}|\leq C_{\delta}(\Xi_{-1}e_{u,-1}^{1/2})^{-1}\},

(313)

uniformly in
n
, which can be made arbitrarily close to
\mathrm{supp}_{t}\,f
by taking
\Xi_{-1}
large.

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Existence of solutions to equation (
35
)

Let
\Phi w
be a solution to the equation:

\overline{D_{t}}[\Phi w]+T^{\ell}w\nabla_{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}=f\text{ with }(\Phi w)[0]=w_{0}.

(314)

Subtracting the equation for
\Phi\tilde{w}
from
\Phi w
, we get:

\overline{D_{t}}(\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w})+T^{\ell}(w-\tilde{w})\nabla_{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}=0\text{ with }(\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w})[0]=0.

(315)

Let
s\geq 0
be given. Using the notation
[\nabla_{\vec{a}},u_{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla]=\tilde{\sum}\mathbf{1}_{|\vec{a}_{2}|\leq s-1}(\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}u^{j}_{\epsilon}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{2}}\nabla_{j})
,
4
4

4

We only want to take up to
s
derivatives of
\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w}
.
we differentiate the equation with
\nabla_{\vec{a}}
to get:

\overline{D_{t}}\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w})+[\nabla_{\vec{a}},u_{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla](\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w})\ +\underset{\vec{a}_{1},\vec{a}_{2}}{\overset{\sim}{\sum}}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}T^{\ell}(w-\tilde{w})\nabla_{\vec{a}_{2}}\nabla_{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}=0.

(316)

Multiplying this equation by
\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w})
and integrating by parts,

\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\Phi w)-\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\Phi\tilde{w})\|_{2}^{2}

+\int\left([\nabla_{\vec{a}},u_{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla](\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w})\ +\underset{\vec{a}_{1},\vec{a}_{2}}{\overset{\sim}{\sum}}\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}T^{\ell}(w-\tilde{w})\nabla_{\vec{a}_{2}}\nabla_{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}\right)\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w})\,dx=0,

where

\int u_{\epsilon}^{j}\nabla_{j}(\nabla_{\vec{a}}\Phi w-\nabla_{\vec{a}}\Phi\tilde{w})^{2}\,dx=0

due to the divergence-free property of
u_{\epsilon}.

Integrating on
[0,t]
, and using Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\Phi w)-\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\Phi\tilde{w})\|_{2}(t)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|[\nabla_{\vec{a}},u_{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla](\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w})\|_{2}+\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{1}}T^{\ell}(w-\tilde{w})\nabla_{\vec{a}_{2}}\nabla_{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}\|_{2}\right)d\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim t\|T^{\ell}(w-\tilde{w})\|_{L^{\infty}H^{s}}+t\|u_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}C^{s}}\|\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{s}}.

In the first line, we used Cauchy-Schwarz.
Here, we used that
\|\nabla_{\vec{a}_{2}}\nabla_{\ell}\theta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1
, which is true since
\theta_{\epsilon}
is smooth. Next, note that
T^{\ell}
is bounded on
H^{s}
. Thus we have
\|T^{\ell}(w-\tilde{w})\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\|w-\tilde{w}\|_{H^{s}}
. Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{|\vec{a}|\leq s}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\Phi w)-\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\Phi\tilde{w})\|_{2}(t)\lesssim_{\theta_{\epsilon},u_{\epsilon}}t\|w-\tilde{w}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{s}}+t\|\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{s}}

and indeed, taking a sup over
t\in[-T,T]

\sum_{|\vec{a}|\leq s}\|\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\Phi w)-\nabla_{\vec{a}}(\Phi\tilde{w})\|_{L^{\infty}L^{2}}\lesssim_{\theta_{\epsilon},u_{\epsilon}}T\|w-\tilde{w}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{s}}+T\|\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{s}}.

By taking
T
sufficiently small, we can absorb the last term into the left-hand side. Taking
T
smaller if necessary, we obtain

\|\Phi w-\Phi\tilde{w}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{s}}\leq C\|w-\tilde{w}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{s}},\quad C\in(0,1).

We apply the contraction mapping theorem and conclude that there exists a unique fixed point
w\in L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s}_{x}
of
\Phi
, which solves equation (
35
).

Inspecting the proof, the timescale of existence is bounded from below by

C^{-1}(\max\{\|\theta\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}C^{s+1}},\|u_{\epsilon}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}C^{s}}\})^{-1}.

Consequently, if
\theta
and
u_{\epsilon}
are smooth, the solution is global in time and smooth in the spatial variables.

A.2
The Divergence Form Principle

Let
\lambda>0
, and let
P_{\lambda,1}
and
P_{\lambda,2}
be frequency-localizing operators with smooth multipliers
\chi_{\lambda,1}
and
\chi_{\lambda,2}
supported where
c\lambda\leq|\xi|\leq C\lambda
. Thus

\widehat{P_{\lambda,i}f}(\xi)=\chi_{\lambda,i}(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi),\qquad\chi_{\lambda,i}(\xi)=\chi_{1,i}(\xi/\lambda).

Choose an even function
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})
that equals one on an open neighborhood of
\mathrm{supp}\,\chi_{1,1}\cup\mathrm{supp}\,\chi_{1,2}
, and define

m_{\lambda}(\xi):=\chi(\xi/\lambda)m(\xi).

When
m
is the SQG symbol, we choose
\chi
radial. The construction below follows
[
31
, Section 4.5.2, equations (15)–(21)]
and
[
32
, Section 5, equations (40)–(45)]
, both building on a cancellation observed in
[
7
]
.

Theorem 5
.

Let
{\cal O}
be an operator with odd symbol
m
that is degree
\beta
homogeneous and smooth away from
0
. Then for smooth
f,g
one can write

\displaystyle P_{\lambda,1}f{\cal O}P_{\lambda,2}g

\displaystyle+P_{\lambda,2}g{\cal O}P_{\lambda,1}f=\nabla_{j}[K_{\lambda}^{j}\ast[f,g]]

(317)

\displaystyle K_{\lambda}^{j}\ast[f,g]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x-h_{1})g(x-h_{2})K_{\lambda}^{j}(h_{1},h_{2})dh_{1}dh_{2}

(318)

\displaystyle K_{\lambda}^{j}(h_{1},h_{2})

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2d+\beta-1}K_{0}^{j}(\lambda h_{1},\lambda h_{2})

where
K_{0}^{j}
is Schwartz. In the specific case in which
{\cal O}=T^{\ell}
is the SQG multiplier, the tensor
K_{\lambda}^{j\ell}
is trace free and satisfies

\displaystyle\widehat{K}_{\lambda}^{j\ell}(p,-p)

\displaystyle=-i\nabla^{j}m^{\ell}(p)

(319)

for all
p
such that
\chi_{\lambda,1}(p)=\chi_{\lambda,2}(-p)=1
.
More precisely, if
Q^{\ell}
denotes the left hand side of (
317
) for
\mathcal{O}=T^{\ell}
, then

\displaystyle Q^{\ell}=\nabla_{j}(K_{\lambda}^{j\ell}\ast[f,g]),\qquad\nabla_{\ell}Q^{\ell}=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{\ell}(K_{\lambda,\mathrm{sym}}^{j\ell}\ast[f,g]),

where
K_{\lambda,\mathrm{sym}}^{j\ell}:=(K_{\lambda}^{j\ell}+K_{\lambda}^{\ell j})/2
. Thus symmetrization preserves the double-divergence identity used in our application, although it need not preserve the vector identity for
Q^{\ell}
. Finally, when
p
is such that
\chi=1
in a neighborhood of
p/\lambda
, we have the formula

\displaystyle\widehat{K}_{\lambda,\mathrm{sym}}^{j\ell}(p,-p)

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2}\big(\nabla^{j}m^{\ell}(p)+\nabla^{\ell}m^{j}(p)\big).

(320)

Proof.

By the approximation in
[
32
, Section 5, before equation (41)]
, it suffices to obtain the divergence form on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for Schwartz functions
f,g
. Let
Q
denote the left hand side of (
317
). Since
m_{\lambda}=m
on the supports of both localizers, convolution in Fourier space gives

\displaystyle\hat{Q}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}[m(\xi-\eta)+m(\eta)]\widehat{P_{\lambda,1}f}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{P_{\lambda,2}g}(\eta)\,d\eta

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}[m_{\lambda}(\xi-\eta)+m_{\lambda}(\eta)]\widehat{P_{\lambda,1}f}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{P_{\lambda,2}g}(\eta)\,d\eta.

The evenness of
\chi
and oddness of
m
imply that
m_{\lambda}
is odd. Hence

\displaystyle m_{\lambda}(\xi-\eta)+m_{\lambda}(\eta)

\displaystyle=m_{\lambda}(\xi-\eta)-m_{\lambda}(-\eta)=\xi_{j}\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{j}m_{\lambda}\big(\sigma\xi-\eta\big)\,d\sigma.

Hence, we obtain a divergence form

\displaystyle\hat{Q}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\xi_{j}\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{j}m_{\lambda}(\sigma\xi-\eta)d\sigma\widehat{P_{\lambda,1}f}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{P_{\lambda,2}g}(\eta)\,d\eta

\displaystyle\hat{Q}(\xi)

\displaystyle=i\xi_{j}\widehat{U^{j}}(\xi)

Taking the inverse Fourier transform of
\widehat{U^{j}}(\xi)
as in
[
32
, equations (41)–(44)]
using
\zeta\equiv\xi-\eta
as an integration variable gives (
318
), with kernel defined by

\displaystyle\widehat{K_{\lambda}^{j}}(\zeta,\eta)

\displaystyle=-i\,\chi_{\lambda,1}(\zeta)\chi_{\lambda,2}(\eta)\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{j}m_{\lambda}\big(\sigma\zeta-(1-\sigma)\eta\big)\,d\sigma.

The Fourier-side kernel is smooth and compactly supported because
m_{\lambda}
is smooth on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. It therefore has a Schwartz inverse Fourier transform. Homogeneity of
m
and the change of variables
(\zeta,\eta)=\lambda(\zeta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
give

K_{\lambda}^{j}(h_{1},h_{2})=\lambda^{2d+\beta-1}K_{0}^{j}(\lambda h_{1},\lambda h_{2}).

At
(\zeta,\eta)=(p,-p)
, the segment in the kernel formula is the single point
p
. The plateau property that
\chi=1
where
\chi_{\lambda,1}(p)=\chi_{\lambda,2}(-p)
are supported therefore gives
\nabla m_{\lambda}(p)=\nabla m(p)
in this range and hence the stated diagonal identity. In the SQG case, radiality of
\chi
gives
\nabla_{j}m_{\lambda}^{j}=0
, so
K_{\lambda}^{j\ell}
is trace free. Finally, contraction of two derivatives with the antisymmetric part of
K_{\lambda}^{j\ell}
vanishes, which proves the double-divergence statement and (
320
).
∎

We also use a version of this principle for even multipliers.

Theorem 6
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an operator with even symbol
m
that is degree
\beta
homogeneous and smooth away from
0
. Then for smooth
f,g
one can write

\displaystyle P_{\lambda,1}f\mathcal{E}P_{\lambda,2}g

\displaystyle-P_{\lambda,2}g\mathcal{E}P_{\lambda,1}f=\nabla_{j}[K_{\lambda}^{j}\ast[f,g]]

(321)

\displaystyle K_{\lambda}^{j}\ast[f,g]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x-h_{1})g(x-h_{2})K_{\lambda}^{j}(h_{1},h_{2})dh_{1}dh_{2}

\displaystyle K_{\lambda}^{j}(h_{1},h_{2})

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2d+\beta-1}K_{0}^{j}(\lambda h_{1},\lambda h_{2})

where the
K_{0}^{j}
are Schwartz functions.

What is crucial here is the minus sign in (
321
) instead of the plus sign in (
317
). With the corrected order of the localizers, the Fourier multiplier is
m(\eta)-m(\xi-\eta)=m(-\eta)-m(\xi-\eta)
, which is factored by
\xi
along the same segment as above.

\tau=b(\log\widehat{\Xi})^{-1}(\Xi e_{u}^{1/2})^{-1}
.

After equation (

\accentset{\ast}{\tau}^{-1}=(N\Xi)^{3/2}D_{R}^{1/2}
.
